                - Food Shortages: Cash crops displaced grains, worsening famines (e.g., 1943 Bengal, 2-3 million deaths).
            - Additional Detail
                - Tea plantations (Assam, 1830s) employed ~1 million indentured workers by 1900, under brutal conditions, per Lucassen’s labor studies.
        - Deindustrialization
            - Definition
                - Collapse of India’s manufacturing due to policies favoring British imports and raw material exports.
            - Causes
                - Import Flood: 1813 Charter Act removed trade barriers; British cotton imports soared, taxed at 2% vs. Indian exports at 10-30%.
                - Patronage Loss: Mughal courts’ decline slashed demand for crafts (e.g., Dhaka muslins).
                - No Modernization: Britain blocked Indian factories, keeping crafts manual.
                - Railways: Enabled British goods’ rural reach and raw material extraction.
            - Mechanisms
                - Textiles: Pre-1780s, weavers sold freely; post-1780s, gomasthas’ Khatbandi system forced sales to Company at low rates.
                - Shipbuilding: Post-1820s, British policies favored English yards; Wadia’s Bombay docks declined.
            - Outcomes
                - Ruralization: Artisans joined agriculture; farming population grew from 70 million (1881) to 100 million (1931), industrial workers fell from 21 million to 13 million, per census data.
                - Trade Shift: Cotton cloth exports dropped from 81% (1750) to 3.7% (1850); raw cotton, opium dominated, per R.C. Dutt’s Economic History.
                - Social Distress: Unemployed artisans fueled 1857 Revolt, reflecting economic grievances.
            - Additional Detail
                - India’s textile exports, once rivaling China’s silk, collapsed as Manchester’s mills produced 50 times faster, per Eric Hobsbawm.

    - Phases of Economic Exploitation (R.P. Dutt’s Framework)
        - Mercantile Phase (1757-1813)
            - Aims
                - Trade Monopoly: Company controlled textiles, spices.
                - Bullion Solution: Indian goods offset Britain’s silver shortage for Asian trade.
            - Features
                - Plundered Bengal’s treasury (~£18 million by 1770, per Edmund Burke).
                - Forced artisans into low-price contracts, undermining markets.
            - Additional Detail
                - The 1770 Bengal famine (10 million deaths) stemmed from tax hikes, exposing mercantile greed.
        - Industrial Phase (1813-1860)
            - Aims
                - Raw Materials: Cotton, indigo, opium for British factories.
                - Market Creation: Sell British goods in India.
            - Features
                - 1813 Charter Act enforced one-way free trade, crippling textiles.
                - India’s GDP share fell from 24% (1700) to 12% (1870), per Maddison.
            - Additional Detail
                - The 1857 Revolt reflected artisans’ and peasants’ anger over industrial displacement.
        - Financial Phase (1860-1947)
            - Aims
                - Capital Investment: British firms profited from railways, plantations.
                - Debt Servicing: India’s revenues funded British loans, costs.
            - Features
                - Railways (£150 million by 1900) served colonial extraction.
                - Home Charges (£20 million/year by 1900) covered British salaries, pensions, debt.
            - Additional Detail
                - India’s debt, £200 million by 1914, tied revenues to London, limiting local investment.

    - Drain of Wealth Theory
        - Definition
            - Concept that Britain extracted India’s wealth through trade imbalances, taxes, and administrative costs, impoverishing India while enriching the metropole.
        - Origins
            - Articulated by Dadabhai Naoroji in Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (1871), expanded by R.C. Dutt and M.G. Ranade.
        - Mechanisms
            - Home Charges: India paid £20-30 million annually (1900s) for British salaries, pensions, military costs, and debt interest, per Naoroji’s calculations.
            - Trade Imbalance: Exported raw materials (cotton, opium) at low prices; imported British goods at high costs, draining ~5% of India’s GDP yearly.
            - Revenue Extraction: High taxes (e.g., 50-70% of produce) left no surplus for reinvestment.
            - Investment Bias: British capital (£1 billion by 1914) built railways, ports for extraction, not local development.
        - Estimates
            - Naoroji estimated £200-300 million drained annually by 1900, equivalent to 30-40% of India’s revenues, per his speeches in London.
            - William Digby (1901) pegged total drain at £6 billion from 1757-1900, dwarfing India’s economy.
        - Impact
            - Stifled industrialization, deepened poverty, and funded Britain’s global dominance, including its Industrial Revolution, per Eric Hobsbawm.
        - Additional Detail
            - The drain, termed “tribute” by Marx in 1853, was unique to India among colonies, as no reciprocal benefits (e.g., infrastructure) matched the outflow.

    - Nationalist Views on British Economic Policies
        - Dadabhai Naoroji
            - Argued Britain’s “moral drain” impoverished India, contrasting its 1700 prosperity with 1900 destitution. Advocated self-rule to retain wealth, per Poverty and Un-British Rule.
        - R.C. Dutt
            - Criticized revenue systems for ruining agriculture, estimating Bengal’s land tax at 60% of output vs. Mughal-era 20%, in Economic History of India (1901).
        - M.G. Ranade
            - Lamented deindustrialization’s “ruralization,” urging protective tariffs and state-led factories, per Essays on Indian Economics (1898).
        - Madan Mohan Malaviya
            - Blamed British imports for artisans’ unemployment, noting weavers’ wages fell 80% by 1880s, per 1905 Congress speeches.
        - Gopal Krishna Gokhale
            - Highlighted famines as policy failures, estimating 15 million deaths (1876-1900) due to revenue pressures, per 1906 budget critiques.
        - Bal Gangadhar Tilak
            - Linked economic exploitation to cultural decay, advocating Swadeshi to revive industries, per Kesari editorials (1900s).
        - Additional Detail
            - Nationalists’ critiques fueled Congress’s economic resolutions (e.g., 1885 demand for tax reform), shaping Swadeshi (1905) and Non-Cooperation (1920) movements.

    - Poverty Under British Rule
        - Scale
            - By 1900, ~80% of Indians lived below subsistence, per Naoroji’s estimates, with per capita income at £2/year vs. Britain’s £30.
            - National income stagnated at ~£500 million (1870-1947), while population grew from 250 to 400 million, per Sivasubramonian’s data.
        - Causes
            - Revenue Burdens: Taxes consumed 50-70% of peasant income, leaving no savings.
            - Deindustrialization: Artisans’ unemployment swelled rural labor, halving wages (e.g., weavers earned 2 annas/day by 1880s).
            - Famines: 30 million died in major famines (1770-1943), worsened by cash crop shifts and British inaction, per Amartya Sen.
            - Trade Drain: Wealth outflow reduced capital for local growth, locking India in agrarian poverty.
        - Regional Patterns
            - Bengal: Jotedar dominance and indigo coercion impoverished ryots; 1943 famine killed 2-3 million.
            - Deccan: Ryotwari taxes and 1876-78 famine (5 million deaths) deepened landlessness.
            - Punjab: Canal colonies benefited elites, but small farmers faced debt, per Imran Ali’s studies.
        - Social Impact
            - Malnutrition rose; life expectancy fell to 23 years by 1921 (vs. 40 in 1700).
            - Caste and gender gaps widened, with landless laborers (often Dalits) and women hit hardest.
        - Additional Detail
            - The 1901 Famine Commission admitted 25% of India’s population was “chronically hungry,” yet relief (e.g., workhouses) offered starvation wages (1 lb grain/day).

    - Economic and Social Consequences
        - Land Fragmentation
            - Cause: Deindustrialization pushed artisans into farming, subdividing plots. Average holdings shrank to ~2 acres in Bengal by 1900, per Famine Commission.
            - Impact: Lower yields, tenancy disputes, rural poverty; ~30% of farmers were landless by 1931.
            - Additional Detail: Fragmentation empowered Jotedars, who leased tiny plots at high rents, fueling 1859 revolts.
        - Middlemen Exploitation
            - Role: Moneylenders, gomasthas, sub-zamindars profited from revenue and crop sales.
            - Impact: Peasants faced usurious loans (20-100% interest); ~25% of Bengal’s farmland mortgaged by 1880s.
            - Additional Detail: The 1875 Deccan riots targeted moneylenders’ debt records, reflecting widespread anger.
        - Famines and Distress
            - Frequency: Major famines—1770 Bengal (10 million deaths), 1876-78 Deccan (5 million), 1896-97 North India (5 million), 1943 Bengal (2-3 million)—killed ~30 million total.
            - Causes: Revenue pressures, cash crops, British inaction (e.g., Churchill’s 1943 rice exports).
            - Additional Detail: Lytton’s 1878 famine policy prioritized exports over relief, sparking nationalist critiques.
        - Trade Imbalance
            - Shift: From 18th-century textile surplus to 19th-century raw material exports and British imports.
            - Impact: India’s global trade share fell from 15% (1700) to 2% (1900), per Paul Bairoch.
            - Additional Detail: Opium exports (£10 million/year by 1850s) balanced Britain’s China trade, tying India to colonial drug networks.
        - Social Polarization
            - Outcome: Absentee landlordism and urban migration widened gaps. Calcutta’s bhadralok contrasted with indebted ryots.
            - Additional Detail: The 1905 Bengal Partition protests reflected economic divides, uniting elites and peasants against British policies.

    - Economic Legacies in Independent India
        - Legacy of Poverty
            - Scale: At independence (1947), ~70% of Indians lived below poverty, with per capita income at Rs. 250/year (~$50), per Planning Commission data.
            - Causes
                - Colonial stagnation left India’s GDP growth at 0.5% annually (1870-1947), vs. 2% pre-1750.
                - Landlessness (40% of rural households) and low wages persisted, hampering savings.
                - Famines eroded human capital; 1943 Bengal famine’s orphans swelled urban slums.
            - Impact
                - Post-1947, India faced food shortages (e.g., 1950s PL-480 imports) and low literacy (18% in 1951), delaying development.
                - Poverty fueled social unrest, like Telangana peasant revolts (1946-51), demanding land reform.
            - Additional Detail
                - Nehru’s 1950s Five-Year Plans prioritized industry to counter poverty, but rural neglect sparked critiques from Ambedkar, who linked caste to economic exclusion.
        - Agrarian Imbalances
            - Issue: Colonial revenue systems left fragmented holdings (average 1.5 acres by 1950) and unequal ownership; 10% of landlords held 50% of land, per 1951 census.
            - Impact
                - Zamindari abolition (1950s) redistributed land slowly; tenant exploitation persisted.
                - Green Revolution (1960s) benefited Punjab but bypassed eastern India, deepening regional disparities.
            - Additional Detail
                - Bihar’s 1970s land reform failures, rooted in Jotedar resistance, fueled Naxalite movements, reflecting colonial legacies.
        - Industrial Underdevelopment
            - Issue: Deindustrialization left India with ~1% of global manufacturing by 1947, vs. 15% in 1700, per Bairoch.
            - Impact
                - Weak industrial base forced reliance on imports; India’s steel output was 1 million tons (1947) vs. Britain’s 13 million.
                - Post-1947 public sector (e.g., SAIL, BHEL) aimed to rebuild industry, but faced capital shortages.
            - Additional Detail
                - TISCO (1907), a rare colonial-era Indian firm, inspired Swadeshi but couldn’t scale without state support, a gap addressed by Nehru’s plans.
        - Infrastructural Bias
            - Issue: Colonial railways (54,000 km by 1947) and ports served extraction, not integration; rural roads covered <5% of villages.
            - Impact
                - Post-1947, India invested heavily in roads (PMGSY, 2000s) and irrigation to counter urban bias.
                - Uneven growth favored Bombay, Calcutta over interior regions like Odisha.
            - Additional Detail
                - The 1956 Industrial Policy Resolution prioritized balanced growth, but colonial port-centric trade delayed hinterland development.
        - Fiscal and Trade Constraints
            - Issue: Colonial drain left India with £1 billion debt (1947) and a weak rupee, devalued 30% in 1949.
            - Impact
                - Foreign exchange shortages forced import controls (1950s), delaying industrialization.
                - Export reliance on tea, jute persisted, limiting diversification until 1980s reforms.
            - Additional Detail
                - The 1991 liberalization traced roots to colonial trade imbalances, as India sought global integration to overcome inherited deficits.
        - Social and Economic Inequality
            - Issue: Colonial policies entrenched landlordism, caste hierarchies, and urban-rural divides.
            - Impact
                - Post-1947, affirmative action (e.g., reservations) tackled caste disparities, but rural poverty (50% below poverty line, 1970s) lingered.
                - Women’s low workforce participation (15% in 1951) reflected colonial neglect of education.
            - Additional Detail
                - Ambedkar’s 1948 Constitution drafting emphasized economic justice to counter colonial-era inequities, shaping India’s welfare policies.

    - Broader Perspectives
        - Global Context
            - India’s economic decline mirrored other colonies (e.g., China’s opium trade), but its drain was unique, funding Britain’s Industrial Revolution, per Immanuel Wallerstein’s world-systems theory.
            - India’s 2% global GDP share by 1947 contrasted with rising powers like the US (25%), underscoring colonial exploitation’s scale.
        - Environmental Impact
            - Colonial forest laws (1878, 1927) and cash crop monocultures degraded soil; ~30% of India’s forests were cleared by 1900, per Ramachandra Guha.
            - Post-1947, India faced desertification and water shortages, addressed by 1970s afforestation programs.
        - Cultural Shifts
            - Deindustrialization eroded artisanal pride, weakening guild traditions; Swadeshi revived khadi to restore dignity, per Gandhi’s 1920s campaigns.
            - Colonial education prioritized clerks over technicians, delaying scientific growth until IITs (1950s).
        - Resistance and Agency
            - Economic exploitation fueled revolts (1857, 1859 Indigo, 1875 Deccan) and Swadeshi, shaping nationalist economics.
            - Post-1947, India’s mixed economy balanced colonial critiques (socialism) with global integration, per Manmohan Singh’s 1991 reforms.
        - Additional Detail
            - The colonial legacy’s complexity—exploitation alongside railways, English education—sparked debates; historians like Amiya Bagchi see it as deliberate underdevelopment, while Niall Ferguson credits infrastructure, reflecting polarized views.

            
- Socio-Religious Reform Movements
    - Understanding Socio-Religious Reform Movements
        - Aim
            - To transform society and religion by addressing social evils and promoting enlightenment for overall improvement.
        - Key Issues Addressed
            - Women's Emancipation (liberation): 
                - Abolition of practices like sati, infanticide, and child marriage, alongside advocacy for widow remarriage.
            - Caste Reform: 
                - Eradication of caste discrimination and untouchability to promote social equality.
            - Promotion of Education: 
                - Fostered (encouraged) societal enlightenment through widespread education initiatives.
            - Religious Reforms: 
                - Combated idolatry and polytheism, and countered exploitation by priests to purify religious practices.
        - Factors Leading to Socio-Religious Reforms
            - Contact with the West: 
                - Exposure to modern ideas of liberty, equality, and democracy influenced India's emerging middle class.
            - Christian Missionary Efforts: 
                - The Charter Act of 1813 permitted missionaries to critique Indian religious practices, sparking reformist responses.
            - Education: 
                - Indian and European scholars fostered pride in India's heritage, empowering reform movements through knowledge and awareness.
        - Reform Strategies and Approaches
            - Reforms from Within: 
                - Advocated self-initiated changes through raising societal awareness.
            - Reforms through Legislation: 
                - Sought legal solutions to social issues, though colonial support was often limited.
            - Reforms through Symbols of Change: 
                - Employed radical actions, such as those by the Derozians, to symbolize transformation.
            - Reforms through Social Work: 
                - Combined practical efforts with intellectual endeavors to effect change.

    - Types of Reformers
        - Revivalists: 
            - Aimed to restore religion to its original greatness, exemplified by movements like Wahabi and Deobandi.
        - Reformists: 
            - Reshaped society with modern ideas, as seen in organizations like Brahmo Samaj and the Aligarh movement.

    - Eastern India
        - Raja Ram Mohan Roy and Brahmo Samaj
            - Known as the Father of Modern India for his pioneering reform efforts.
            - Key Contributions
                - Authored “Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin” (A Gift to Monotheists) in Persian in 1805, promoting monotheistic ideas.
                - Founded Atmiya Sabha in 1815 to critique idolatry and polytheism, advocating monotheism and rational thought.
                - Established the Calcutta Unitarian Committee in 1823 with Dwarka Nath Tagore, uniting prominent Brahmins and European residents to promote Hindu social reforms.
                - Influenced the abolition of sati in 1829 with Governor-General William Bentinck's support, drawing on Dayabhaga and Mitakshara legal schools, and championed widow rights and women's education.
                - Supported the establishment of Hindu College in 1817 to advance education.
                - Founded Vedanta College in 1825 to blend Western and Indian sciences for a holistic education.
                - Translated Vedas and Upanishads into accessible languages and published Sambad Kaumudi (Bengali) and Mirat-ul-Akbar (Persian) to spread reformist ideas.
            - Brahmo Samaj (1828)
                - Focused on monotheism, social equality, and human dignity as core principles.
        - Evolution of Brahmo Samaj After Raja Ram Mohan Roy
            - After Roy's death in 1833, leadership passed to Debendranath Tagore, father of Rabindranath Tagore.
            - Debendranath Tagore's Contributions
                - Founded Tattvabodhini Sabha in 1839 to promote rationalism, focusing on India's history and indigenous languages, continuing Roy's legacy.
                - Worked to reform Hinduism internally by eliminating idolatry and irrational customs, while resisting Christian missionaries' critiques and conversion efforts.
                - Expanded Brahmo Samaj's influence beyond Bengal to regions like the United Provinces, Punjab, Bombay, and Madras.
            - Keshab Chandra Sen's Role
                - A prominent reformer who joined Brahmo Samaj in the mid-19th century, bringing new directions.
                - Established the Tabernacle of New Dispensation in 1867 to integrate Hinduism, Islam, and Christianity into a unified spiritual framework.
                - Founded the Indian Reform Association in 1870 to address women's rights, caste discrimination, and education.
                - His radical ideas, including support for inter-caste marriages and teachings from all religions, led to a split in 1866: Brahmo Samaj of India (led by Keshab Chandra Sen) and Adi Brahmo Samaj (led by Debendranath Tagore).
            - Sadharan Brahmo Samaj (1878)
                - Emerged after Keshab Chandra Sen conducted his underage daughter's marriage using orthodox rituals, contradicting reformist principles.
                - Disgruntled members formed this group, focusing on original Brahmo ideals: faith in a singular supreme being, emphasis on reason and morality, and opposition to orthodox practices.
                - 
        - Henry Vivian Derozio and Young Bengal Movement
            - Derozio: An Anglo-Indian radical thinker and professor at Hindu College, Kolkata, who founded the Academic Association and championed free thought, liberty, equality, and fraternity.
            - Young Bengal Movement: Influenced by Western thinkers, it promoted Raja Ram Mohan Roy's ideas, emphasizing education, press freedom, and justice.
        - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
            - Key Contributions
                - Reformed Sanskrit education by admitting non-Brahmins to Sanskrit College in 1851.
                - Led the Hindu Widow Remarriage Act of 1856 to legalize widow remarriage.
                - Advocated women's education and established schools for girls.
                - Authored Barna Parichay, a foundational text for modern Bengali grammar, significantly contributing to societal reform.

    - Western India
        - Balshastri Jambhekar
            - Known as the "Father of Marathi Journalism" for his pioneering media work.
            - Key Contributions: Founded Darpan, the first Marathi newspaper, in 1832.
            - Social Reforms: Opposed caste discrimination, sati, and child marriage, while advocating women's education and widow remarriage.
        - Manav Dharma Sabha
            - Founded in Surat in 1844 by Durgaram Manchharam, Dadoba Pandurang, and Dalpatram Bhagubai.
            - Focus: Challenged caste systems and societal superstitions through weekly meetings discussing rationalism and equality.
        - Paramahansa Mandali
            - Established in Bombay in 1849 by Ram Bal Krishna Jayakar and Dadoba Pandurang as a radical socio-religious group.
            - Teachings: Advocated monotheism and morality over rituals, opposed caste discrimination, and supported widow remarriage and women's education.
        - Prarthana Samaj
            - Founded by Atmaram Pandurang in Bombay in 1867, influenced by Brahmo Samaj ideals.
            - Goals: Promoted women's education and the abolition of caste discrimination.
        - Jyotirao Phule and the Satyashodhak Samaj
            - Jyotirao Phule: Criticized orthodox Brahmins and caste dominance, advocating education for women and lower castes, earning the title “Mahatma” for his social reform works. Published Gulamgiri and other equality-focused writings.
            - Savitribai Phule (Wife): Opened India's first school for girls in 1851 and founded Mahila Seva Mandal in 1852.
            - Satyashodhak Samaj (1873): Promoted inter-caste marriages, elimination of child marriage, widow remarriage, and education for marginalized sections to achieve societal equality.
        - Gopal Hari Deshmukh (Lokhitwadi)
            - A prominent social reformer and writer from Maharashtra.
            - Key Contributions: Advocated modern human values and criticized social evils through his journal Prabhakar (1848-1889), supported the Swadeshi movement for self-reliance, and championed women's education while opposing caste discrimination and untouchability.
        - Gopal Ganesh Agarkar
            - Co-founded New English School (1880) in Poona, Deccan Education Society (1884), and Fergusson College (1885).
            - Advocated for the Age of Consent Act (1891) to raise the legal marriage age, worked for women's rights, opposed child marriage, and supported legislative reforms over traditional methods.
        - Maharshi Dhondo Keshav Karve
            - Known as a pioneer for widow remarriage and women's education.
            - Key Achievements: Founded Vidhwa Vivah Uttejak Mandali (1893) to encourage widow remarriage, Hindu Widows Home Association (1896) for shelter and education, and SNDT Women's University (1916), India's first women's university in Maharashtra. Advocated women's self-reliance through education and vocational training, married a widow after his first wife's death as an example, and was honored with the Bharat Ratna in 1958.
        - Servants of India Society
            - Founded by Gopal Krishna Gokhale in 1905 at Poona.
            - Core Ideals: Promoted selfless service to the underprivileged, rural communities, and tribal populations, focusing on education, sanitation, and health care reforms.
            - Gopal Krishna Gokhale: Leader of the moderate Indian National Congress faction, mentored Mahatma Gandhi, coined the phrase “What Bengal thinks today, India thinks tomorrow,” and published The Hitavada (Nagpur, 1911) to spread reformist ideas.
        - Behramji Malabari
            - A Parsi social reformer focused on women's rights.
            - Key Contributions: Advocated for the Age of Consent Act (1891) to raise the marriageable age, founded Seva Sadan (1908) for shelter, education, and vocational training for destitute women, and inspired change through writings like The Indian Muse in English Garb.
        - Pandita Ramabai
            - First Indian woman awarded “Pandita” and “Saraswati” titles by the University of Calcutta for her scholarship.
            - Reforms and Contributions: Founded Sharada Sadan (Bombay) for widows and Mukti Mission (1890s) in Pune as a Christian charity for destitute women, toured the US to raise funds for widows, authored Stri Dharma Niti (Morals for Women) in 1882 and The High-Caste Hindu Woman critiquing child marriage and widow exploitation, and received the Kaisar-i-Hind Medal in 1919.
        - Rukhmabai Case
            - Rukhmabai (1864-1955): A physician and feminist, one of India's first women doctors.
            - Legal Battle: Fought against child marriage in Rukhmabai vs. Dadaji Bhikaji (1885), refusing to live with her husband, resulting in a landmark verdict questioning child marriage legality and highlighting women's rights.
        - Social Service League
            - Founded by N.M. Joshi in Bombay in 1911 to promote social welfare.
        - Vishnushastri Chiplunkar
            - Co-founded Mahratta and Kesari newspapers with Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Gopal Ganesh Agarkar to advocate reform and nationalism.

    - Pan India
        - Ramakrishna Movement
            - Founded by Ramakrishna Paramahansa (1834-1886).
            - Core Teachings: All religions lead to the same goal, emphasizing spiritual equality and service to mankind as service to God.
            - Objectives: Establish a monastic order for spiritual pursuit and engage in philanthropic and charitable works across caste, color, and creed.
            - Paramahansa's disciples, led by Vivekananda, formed Ramakrishna Math in 1887 and Ramakrishna Mission in 1897, headquartered at Belur near Calcutta.
        - Swami Vivekananda (Narendra Nath Dutta)
            - Disciple of Ramakrishna Paramahansa and key Vedantic philosophy proponent.
            - Key Contributions: Represented Hinduism at the World Parliament of Religions (1893) in Chicago, elevating India's spiritual legacy, founded Ramakrishna Mission (1897) for education, health care, and social welfare, established Belur Math as its headquarters, and published Prabuddha Bharata (monthly in English) and Udbodhan (fortnightly in Bengali).
        - Swami Dayanand Saraswati and Arya Samaj
            - Founder: Swami Dayananda Saraswati (originally Mool Shankar Tiwari), established Arya Samaj in Bombay in 1875 with the slogan “Go Back to the Vedas.”
            - Ideology: Opposed idolatry, ritualism, and priesthood, advocated returning to Vedic principles as ultimate truth, opposed child marriages, and encouraged widow remarriage.
            - Key Contributions: Authored Satyarth Prakash (The Light of Truth), coined “India for Indians” in 1876 (known as Martin Luther of India), wrote Veda Bhasya and Veda Bhasya Bhumika, introduced the Shuddhi Movement for reconversion of Hindus (Ghar Wapsi), and promoted cow protection via Gokarunanidhi.
            - Post-Dayananda Era
                - Divisions: College Party (led by Lala Hansraj, Lala Lajpat Rai, and Lala Lal Chand) focused on modern education through DAV schools and colleges (e.g., DAV College, Lahore); Mahatma Party (later Gurukul Party, led by Swami Shraddhanand and Guru Datta Vidyarthi) emphasized Sanskrit and Vedic education via Kanya Mahavidyalaya for widows and Gurukuls.
                - Arya Mahila Samaj (Arya Women Society): Founded by Pandita Ramabai in 1882.
        - Indian Social Conference
            - Founded in 1887 by M.G. Ranade and Raghunath Rao as the social reform cell of the Indian National Congress, addressing caste discrimination and women's rights.
        - The Theosophical Society
            - Founded in 1875 in New York by Madame Helena Blavatsky and Colonel H.S. Olcott, shifted headquarters to Adyar, Madras, in 1882.
            - Key Tenets: Promoted brotherhood without distinction of race, creed, or gender, studied religion and philosophy concurrently, and investigated nature's hidden laws and mysticism.
            - Social Reform Work: Advocated caste abolition and untouchability eradication, founded Central Hindu School (1898) in Varanasi, which evolved into Banaras Hindu University led by Madan Mohan Malviya.
        - Radha Swami Satsang
            - Founded in 1861 by Shiv Dayal Saheb, emphasizing belief in one supreme being, spiritual equality, rejection of idol worship, and focus on moral living and ethical practices.
        - Dev Samaj
            - Founded by Shiv Narayan Agnihotri in 1887, focused on caste integration, women's education, and moral reform, with teachings compiled in Dev Shastra.
        - Bharat Dharma Mahamandal
            - Founded in 1902 in Varanasi to unify organizations like Sanatana Dharma Sabha and Dharma Maha Parishad, defending Hinduism against reformist movements like Arya Samaj and Theosophists.

    - Caste Reform Movements
        - Causes for the Rise of Caste Movements
            - Grievances of Educated Men: 
                - Discrimination by upper castes and challenges to centuries-old caste-based inequality.
            - Social Mobility Aspirations: 
                - Lower castes sought upward movement through Sanskritization, adopting upper-caste customs, manners, and taboos.
            - Radical Leaders: 
                - Figures like Jyotirao Phule and Periyar opposed Brahminical dominance.
            - British Interventions: 
                - The 1901 Census categorized castes hierarchically, reinforcing rigid social divisions.
        - Caste Reform Movements in South India
            - Ayya Vaikundar (Tamil Nadu)
                - Advocated equality and fraternity, abolishing caste-based discrimination.
                - Contributions: Simplified marriage customs without Sanskrit rituals, organized Sampanthi Bhojana (community meals) for diverse castes, dug common wells for lower castes, and inspired priests to apply sacred paste to all regardless of caste.
            - Ezhava Movement (Kerala)
                - Founder: Sree Narayana Guru Swamy among the Ezhava community.
                - Actions: Installed a Shivalinga in 1888 to challenge higher-caste monopoly, led Aruvippuram Kshetra Yogam (1889) for temple entry, education, and government representation for Ezhavas.
                - Legacy: Formed SNDP (Sree Narayana Dharma Paripalana Yogam) for rights to school admission, government jobs, road access, temple entry, and political representation.
            - Palli Movement (Tamil Nadu)
                - Focused on lower-caste Pallis adopting Kshatriya customs for social upliftment.
            - Vokkaliga Sangha (Mysore Region)
                - Founded in 1905 as an anti-Brahmin movement, promoting social, cultural, and educational upliftment of the Vokkaliga community.
            - Nadar Movement (Tamil Nadu)
                - Focused on toddy-tappers achieving Kshatriya status, established Nadar Mahajana Sangam in 1910.
            - Justice Movement (Madras)
                - Leaders: C.N. Mudaliar, T.M. Nair, P. Tyagaraja Chetti.
                - Demands: Greater representation in government services and politics for intermediate castes.
            - Self-Respect Movement (E.V. Ramaswamy Periyar)
                - Advocated weddings without Brahmin priests, forcible temple entry, and rejection of Manusmriti.
            - Periyar (E.V. Ramaswamy Naicker) - Tamil Nadu
                - Ideology: Rejected Brahminical culture and superstitions, promoted Dravidian leadership via Dravida Kazhagam, and advocated atheism, education, and anti-caste reforms.
                - Symbolism: Used black shirts and flags to protest caste hierarchies.
            - Vaikom Satyagraha (1924) (Kerala)
                - Leaders: K.P. Kesava, Sree Narayana Guru, T.K. Madhavan.
                - Objectives: Demanded temple entry for Dalits, supported by Gandhi, leading to state-managed temples opening to all in 1936.
        - Caste Reform Movements in Rest of India
            - Gopal Baba Walangkar (Maharashtra)
                - Founded Anarya Dosh-Parihar Mandali in 1886, challenged untouchability and caste oppression through writings like Vital Viddhwansak.
            - Kisan Faghu Bansode (Maharashtra)
                - Founded Antyaj Samaj for Dalit education, wrote plays, poems, and articles advocating emancipation.
            - Shivram Janba Kamble (Maharashtra)
                - Edited Somvanshiya Mitra, focusing on the Dalit cause, and organized India's first Dalit conference.
            - Maharshi Vitthal Ramji Shinde (Maharashtra)
                - Founded India Depressed Classes Mission in 1906, advocated untouchability eradication and Dalit upliftment, established the “All India Untouchability League.”
            - Mahad Movement
                - Led by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar in March 1927, targeting caste-based Hindu traditions. Earlier, in 1924, he founded Bahishkrit Hitkarini Sabha to voice Dalit challenges, guided by the principle “Educate, Agitate, and Organize.”

    - Parsi Reform Movements
        - Rahnomae Mazdayasanan Sabha (1851)
            - Focused on restoring Zoroastrian religion, led by Naoroji Furdonji and Dadabhai Naoroji (Rast Goftar).

    - Muslim Reform Movements
        - Wahhabi Movement (Revivalist)
            - Founder: Syed Ahmad Barelvi.
            - Advocated unity in Islamic interpretation and resistance against British rule and Sikh authority.
        - Fairaizi Movement (Revivalist)
            - Founder: Haji Shariatullah.
            - Aimed to protest un-Islamic practices among Muslims, influenced by the Wahhabi Movement.
        - Ahmadiyya Movement (Punjab) (Reformist)
            - Founder: Mirza Ghulam Ahmad in 1889, promoted universal brotherhood, Western liberal education, and religious tolerance.
        - Syed Ahmed Khan (Reformist)
            - Established Muhammadan Anglo-Oriental College in 1875 (later Aligarh Muslim University), founded the Scientific Society of Aligarh modeled on the Royal Society of London, and sought British support to modernize Muslim society through education and employment post-revolt politics.

    - Sikh Reform Movements
        - Nirankari Movement
            - Founder: Baba Dayal Das (1783-1855).
            - Rejected idol worship, emphasized Guru Nanak's teachings, and opposed evils like meat-eating and liquor consumption.
        - Namdhari Movement
            - Founder: Baba Ram Singh (1816-1885).
            - Objectives: Revitalized Sikhism and enforced strict vegetarianism.
        - Singh Sabha Movement (1873)
            - Led by Thakur Singh Sandhawalia and Giani Gian Singh, established Singh Sabha of Amritsar to counter Christian and Muslim conversion efforts, promote Gurumukhi and Punjabi literature, and founded Khalsa College in Amritsar in 1892.
        - Akali Movement
            - Targeted corrupt Mahants managing Sikh gurdwaras and aimed to restore control of Khalsa College to the Sikh community, supported the Non-Cooperation Movement, and resulted in the Sikh Gurdwara Act of 1925 for democratic management.
            

- People's Resistance Against British Rule
    - Meaning of People's Resistance
        - Definition
            - The term "people" refers to diverse segments of Indian society adversely affected by foreign domination, including peasants, artisans, tribals, ruling classes (both active and dispossessed), military personnel (serving under the Company or demobilized from ex-rulers' armies), and religious leaders (both Hindu and Muslim).
        - Purpose of Resistance
            - To safeguard their interests, these groups fought both independently and in collaboration with others against British rule.

    - Factors for People's Uprisings Against the British
        - Economic and Social Grievances
            - Collapse of indigenous industries due to British economic policies.
            - Exploitation by non-tribal people like moneylenders and traders.
            - Discontent stemming from oppressive land policies.
        - Impact of East India Company's Rule
            - Resentment from zamindars and artisans over lost privileges and livelihoods.
            - Erosion of traditional rights and ownership systems under Company governance.
        - Cultural Factors
            - Desire for autonomy within the Indian cultural and political framework.
            - Resistance to imposed cultural influences like Sanskritization, which threatened local identities.

    - Civil and Peasant Uprisings During British Rule (Pre-1857)
        - Sanyasi Revolt (1760s-1800)
            - Nature of Revolt
                - An uprising by Hindu Sanyasis and Muslim Fakirs in Eastern India, supported by rural impoverished communities and minor zamindars.
            - Causes
                - Religious Restrictions: British restrictions on pilgrimage and taxes on religious donations angered spiritual leaders.
                - Economic Hardship: The Bengal Famine of 1770 worsened conditions, fueling rebellion.
            - Major Areas
                - Purnea, Malda, Dhaka, Rangpur, Cooch Behar, and Murshidabad in Bengal Province.
            - Notable Leaders and Activities
                - Leaders included Bhawani Pathak, Majnu Shah, Chirag Ali, and Musa Shah, who launched surprise raids on British outposts, treasuries, and factories.
            - Outcome
                - Eventually suppressed after a prolonged struggle by British forces.
            - Significance
                - Inspired literary works like "Devi Chaudhurani" and "Anandamath" by Bankim Chandra Chatterjee, reflecting its cultural legacy.
        - Rangpur Dhing (1783)
            - Area of Revolt
                - Rangpur District of Bengal in 1783.
            - Nature of Revolt
                - A protest by peasants and zamindars against excessive revenue demands under the Ijaradari System (land auctioned to the highest bidder).
            - Leader
                - Kena Sarkar.
            - Outcome
                - Highlighted flaws in the Ijaradari System, contributing to reforms like the Permanent Settlement.
        - Paika Rebellion (1817)
            - Location
                - Odisha.
            - Causes
                - Resistance against British policies, particularly high land revenue demands.
            - Leaders
                - Bakshi Jagabandhu Bidyadhar and Mukunda Deva.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by 1818 but led to governance changes, including large remission of arrears, reduced assessments, and a new settlement on fixed tenures.
            - Additional Fact
                - In 2017, Odisha urged recognition as the First War of Independence, though the Indian Council of Historical Research (ICHR) disagreed.
            - Who Were Paikas?
                - Paikas were foot soldiers recruited since the 16th century by Odisha kings for martial services in exchange for hereditary rent-free lands.
        - Mysore Rebellion (1830)
            - Location
                - Mysore (Nagar District).
            - Causes
                - Exploitative taxation after the Wodeyar dynasty's reinstatement and enforcement of subsidiary alliances, burdening peasants and lower societal sections.
            - Leader
                - Sardar Malla.
            - Outcome
                - Resulted in direct British administration of the region.
        - Kuka Movement (1840-1850)
            - Nature
                - Also known as the Namdhari Movement, it aimed at religious purification and political resistance against British rule.
            - Founder
                - Bhagat JawaharMal.
            - Leader
                - Baba Ram Singh, who emphasized military training and structured resistance; Kukas boycotted British goods, institutions, and laws.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by the British; Baba Ram Singh was deported to Rangoon.

    - Peasant Movements with Religious Overtones
        - Narkelberia Uprising (1830s)
            - Leader
                - Syed Mir Nisar Ali (Titu Mir).
            - Nature
                - Muslim tenants rose against Hindu landlords and British indigo planters imposing a bread tax on Faraizi Muslims; initially economic, it merged into the Wahabi movement with religious overtones.
        - The Pagal Panthis (1825-1833)
            - Nature
                - Peasant uprisings led by a religious sect in present-day Bangladesh, initiated by Karam Shah as a religious movement, later turned political under his son Tipu with local tribes.
            - Activities
                - Directed against zamindar oppression, refusing excessive rent and attacking their houses.
            - Outcome
                - Suppressed by British large-scale military operations.
        - Faraizi Revolt (1818-1857)
            - Leaders
                - Haji Shariatullah, later Dudu Mian of the Faraizi sect.
            - Nature
                - Sought to cleanse Islam and support tenants' rights against zamindars; many Faraizis joined the Wahhabi Movement.

    - 

    - Tribal Uprisings During British Rule
        - Overview
            - Tribes in the 18th century lived in isolation with distinct agricultural methods, traditions, and societal structures, mainly in forested lands encroached upon by the British, leading to recurrent uprisings.
            - 
        - Understanding the Evolution of Tribal Movements Under British Rule
            - First Phase (1795-1860)
                - Rise of the British Empire; leadership from traditional sections like zamindars whose privileges were undermined.
            - Second Phase (1860-1920)
                - Intensive colonialism and mercantilism impacted tribal economy, land, and forests; leadership emerged from lower tribal ranks.
            - Third Phase (1920-1947)
                - Tribals engaged in nationalist, agrarian, and separatist movements; leadership from educated tribal leaders and outsiders.
            - 
        - Plight of the Tribals Under Colonial Rule - Key Issues
            - Debt Spirals and Outsider Dominance
                - Traders and moneylenders offered loans for non-local goods, leading to dependency, high interest rates, and debt cycles.
            - Eroded Chieftaincy (From Leaders to Enforcers)
                - Tribal leaders reduced to enforcers of British policies, losing respect among their people.
            - The Agrarian Dilemma (Forced Settlements and Fallow Fields)
                - Forced settlement and farming under unsuitable conditions increased hardships.
            - Forests Under Siege (Land Now State Property of British)
                - Forest laws restricted access, impacting livelihoods, forcing relocation, and reducing autonomy.
            - Labour Traps (Plantations and Mines)
                - Recruitment for tea plantations and mining led to loss of mobility and freedom.
        - Indian Forest Act
            - Background
                - A key conflict source due to timber demand in the 1850s for railways (fuel and sleepers) and shipbuilding (Indian teak for British naval dominance).
            - Forest Policy (1864)
                - Established by Lord Dalhousie, declaring forests state property and enabling British control over tree-containing land.
            - Forest Act of 1878
                - Divided forests into Reserved (fully government-controlled for heavy commercial use), Protected (partly government-controlled), and Village Forests (controlled by nearby villages); tribes lost traditional rights like grazing and agriculture, receiving only limited privileges.
            - Forest Act of 1927
                - Did not acknowledge community rights; individuals or communities had to claim rights before a Forest Officer; traditional practices like shifting agriculture and collecting forest produce were made illegal.
        - Revolt by Mainland Indian Tribes
            - Pahariyas' Rebellion (1778)
                - Area
                    - Rajmahal Hills (Jharkhand).
                - Cause
                    - Protest against British disruption of their lifestyle.
                - Main Tactic
                    - Engaged British in guerrilla warfare.
                - Result
                    - British declared the Pahariya region “Daman-i-Koh” (forbidden territory), agreeing not to extend administration further.
            - Chuar Uprising (1798-1799)
                - Area
                    - Midnapore and Bankura districts of Bengal.
                - Cause
                    - Protest against British revenue policies.
                - Activities
                    - Multiple waves between 1766 and 1798, led by Durjan Singh, a deprived zamindar; also known as the Revolt of Jungle Mahal; brutally suppressed by British.
            - Kol Revolt (1831)
                - Location
                    - Chotanagpur.
                - Cause
                    - Against land transfers to non-tribal settlers and oppressive British colonial policies.
                - Leader
                    - Budho Bhagat, under whom rebels killed about a thousand perceived oppressors.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed with large-scale military operations.
            - Ho and Munda Uprisings (1820-1837)
                - Location
                    - Singhbhum and broader Chotanagpur region.
                - Cause
                    - Resistance to British revenue systems and Bengali settlers.
                - Leadership
                    - Raja of Parahat.
                - Activities
                    - Ho Revolt persisted until 1827 when Ho tribals surrendered; in 1831, they rebelled again with Mundas, subdued by 1837.
            - Koli Uprising (1822-1829)
                - Location
                    - Maharashtra (particularly Akola hills).
                - Cause
                    - British interventions in tribal rights and exploitation by moneylenders and zamindars.
                - Leader
                    - Ramji Bhangre.
                - Activities
                    - Rebels targeted Baniya moneylenders, attacking and looting financial documents; British responded with military force.
            - Ramosi Uprisings (1820s-1830s)
                - Location
                    - Western Ghats.
                - Cause
                    - Protest against livelihood loss after Maratha territory annexation.
                - Leaders
                    - Chittur Singh and Umaji Naik.
                - Activities
                    - Armed attacks on British establishments; brutally suppressed by British.
            - Santhal Rebellion (1855-1856)
                - Area
                    - Santhal Pargana (Jharkhand and West Bengal).
                - Causes
                    - Encroachment by outsiders (Dikus) invading land rights, economic exploitation by zamindars, and loss of traditional lifestyle.
                - Leaders
                    - Sidhu and Kanhu Murmu, who rallied Santhals, believing they were divinely chosen to end oppression and restore their way of life.
                - Tactics
                    - Armed insurrection with traditional weapons.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed by British; Santhal Pargana created as an administrative district in 1855 for some autonomy; the 1876 Santhal Parganas Tenancy Act prohibited land transfer to non-tribals.
            - Khond Uprisings (1837-1856)
                - Causes
                    - Resentment against British control, cultural disruption, loss of territorial rights, abolition of the Meriah System (human sacrifices for fertility), and economic exploitation via taxes and moneylenders.
                - Regions
                    - Hilly tracts of Odisha and Andhra Pradesh.
                - Leader
                    - Chakra Bisoi, uniting tribes like Ghumusar and Kalahandi against British policies.
                - Methods
                    - Used traditional weapons like tangi (axes), bows, arrows, and swords.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed due to superior British military strength.
            - Koya Revolts (1803-1886)
                - Causes
                    - Oppression by police and moneylenders, exploitation under British forest laws.
                - Notable Leaders
                    - Tomma Sora (1879-1880 rebellion) and Raja Anantayyar.
                - Methods
                    - Guerrilla tactics exploiting terrain.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed by British forces.
            - Bhil Revolts
                - Causes
                    - Famine, economic hardship, and British misgovernance, with revolts in 1817, 1825, 1831, 1846, and 1913.
                - Regions
                    - Western Ghats, southern Rajasthan (Banswara district), and Dhule district (Maharashtra), strategically linking central India to the western coast.
                - Notable Events
                    - Sindwa Treasury Loot (1857): Around 500 Bhils, led by Kazi Singh, plundered the treasury.
                    - Mangarh Massacre (1913) (Adivasi Jallianwala): Bhils, inspired by Govind Guru, protested peacefully for land and rights; British killed over 1,500, including women and children.
                - Methods
                    - Guerrilla warfare and exploitation of mountainous terrain for plundering state treasures.
                - Outcome
                    - Revolts suppressed by British.
            - Munda Rebellion (1899-1900) (Ulgulan, meaning Uprising)
                - Region
                    - Chotanagpur region (Jharkhand).
                - Causes
                    - Loss of tribal lands under the Khuntkatti System (collective ownership), seized by non-tribal settlers (Dikus) becoming jagirdars and zamindars, forcing Mundas into landless labor; protected forests deprived forest rights; exploitation via beth begari (forced labor) and missionary interference.
                - Leader
                    - Birsa Munda, who declared himself a messenger of God, started the Birsait faith, blending Hindu-Christian elements into Munda ideology.
                - Methods
                    - Transformed into the “Sardari Fight,” a political and religious campaign to establish Munda Raj.
                - Outcome
                    - Birsa's arrest and death in 1900 ended the movement; the Chotanagpur Tenancy Act (1908) recognized Khuntkatti rights, banned begari, and restricted land transfer to non-tribals.
        - Tribal Uprisings of North East
            - Ahom Revolt (1828)
                - Causes
                    - British refusal to leave Assam after the 1826 Treaty of Yandaboo, contrary to promises post-Anglo-Burmese War.
                - Regions
                    - Sibsagar district, Assam.
                - Leaders
                    - Gomadhar Konwar and Dhanjay Borgohain.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed by British; leaders executed.
            - Anglo-Khasi War (1829-1833)
                - Causes
                    - Khasi resistance to British colonization.
                - Leader
                    - Tirath Singh, who organized an armed revolt.
                - Outcome
                    - Tirath Singh captured and exiled to Dhaka.
            - Singhpo Rebellions (1830, 1843)
                - Regions
                    - Arunachal Pradesh.
                - Leaders
                    - Bom Singhpo (1830): Attacked British armory but was captured.
                    - Beesa Gaum (1843): Led armed resistance but sentenced to life imprisonment.
            - Tai Khamti Resistance (1839-1844)
                - Location
                    - Arunachal Pradesh.
                - Causes
                    - Fear of British-imposed taxes.
                - Leader
                    - Chow Rang Pha Gohain.
                - Outcome
                    - British defeated the Khamtis and asserted control over Arunachal Pradesh.

    - Military Mutinies During British Rule
        - Overview
            - Military mutinies in British India, primarily by Indian sepoys, were significant acts of resistance against colonial authority, driven by cultural, economic, and political grievances. These uprisings, occurring between 1806 and 1857, reflected tensions within the East India Company’s armies, often sparked by insults to religious practices, discriminatory treatment, or fears of annexation. While smaller than the 1857 Revolt, they foreshadowed widespread rebellion, highlighting sepoy discontent and inspiring later nationalist movements, per historians Bipan Chandra and Stanley Wolpert.
            - Objectives
                - Resist British cultural and political impositions within military structures.
                - Protect sepoy rights, traditions, and regional loyalties.
            - Scope
                - Involved ~10,000 sepoys across multiple incidents, impacting British military control, per Company records and Eric Stokes’s Peasant Armed.
            - Context
                - Mutinies arose amid Company’s expansion (~50% of India by 1806), annexation policies (e.g., Awadh, 1856), and economic pressures on sepoys (~20% pay cuts post-1800), per Home Ministry archives.
        - Key Mutinies
            - Vellore Mutiny (1806)
                - Date and Location
                    - July 10, 1806, Vellore Fort, Tamil Nadu.
                - Nature
                    - Early armed revolt by Indian sepoys against British rule, per Madras Army dispatches.
                - Causes
                    - Cultural Interference: British banned religious symbols (e.g., caste marks, turbans) and mandated European hats, violating Hindu and Muslim practices, per General Order 1806.
                    - Resentment: Racial discrimination, poor pay (~7 rupees/month vs. British soldiers’ 20), and sympathy for Tipu Sultan’s family imprisoned in Vellore Fort, per Fort records.
                - The Revolt
                    - Sepoys (~1,500 from 1st and 23rd Regiments) killed 14 British officers, unfurled Tipu Sultan’s flag, and briefly seized the fort, per Colonel Gillespie’s report.
                    - Sparked by a sepoy’s warning to Tipu’s sons, per Company inquiries.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed within hours by British reinforcements under Gillespie; ~800 sepoys killed, 600 executed or imprisoned, per Court of Inquiry 1806.
                    - Tipu’s family relocated to Calcutta to prevent further agitation, per Madras Government orders.
                    - Dress code relaxed, restoring caste marks, per Company reforms.
                - Significance
                    - Precursor to 1857 Revolt, exposed sepoy grievances, and alarmed British (~10% of Madras Army involved), per Wolpert’s History of India.
                - Additional Detail
                    - Mutiny involved ~5% of Vellore’s garrison; public executions deterred further unrest, per Calcutta Gazette 1806.
            - Barrackpore Mutiny (1824)
                - Date and Location
                    - November 2, 1824, Barrackpore, Bengal.
                - Nature
                    - Sepoy rebellion against deployment to the Anglo-Burmese War, per Bengal Army records.
                - Causes
                    - Religious Concerns: Hindu sepoys feared overseas travel (to Burma) violated caste purity, as crossing “Kala Pani” was taboo, per sepoy petitions.
                    - Logistical Grievances: Inadequate transport (no bullocks for baggage), delayed pay (~2 months arrears), per Company correspondence.
                    - Context: Resentment over British disregard for sepoy customs post-Plassey expansion, per Stokes’s studies.
                - The Revolt
                    - 47th Native Infantry (~800 sepoys) refused orders to march, led by Bindee Tiwari, and gathered armed on the parade ground, per Barrackpore logs.
                    - Demanded pay and transport assurances, per eyewitness accounts.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed by British cavalry and artillery; 180 sepoys killed, 11 executed (including Tiwari), 50 imprisoned, per Court Martial 1824.
                    - 47th Regiment disbanded, erasing its colors, per Bengal Army orders.
                    - British introduced transport reforms, per Company directives 1825.
                - Significance
                    - Highlighted sepoy sensitivity to religious customs, influencing 1857’s greased cartridge issue, per Chandra’s India’s Struggle.
                    - Affected ~2% of Bengal Army, per Army estimates.
                - Additional Detail
                    - Mutiny fueled rumors of wider unrest; ~1,000 locals witnessed executions, per Bengal Gazette 1824.
            - Bharatpur Mutiny (1850)
                - Date and Location
                    - January 1850, Bharatpur, Rajasthan.
                - Nature
                    - Sepoy unrest over pay deductions and annexation fears, per North-Western Provinces records.
                - Causes
                    - Economic Grievances: Deductions for uniforms (~10% of pay) amid rising grain prices (~50% hike post-1849 famine), per sepoy complaints.
                    - Political Fears: Rumors of Bharatpur’s annexation after princely disputes, per Company intelligence.
                    - Context: Growing distrust post-Sikh Wars, per Dalhousie’s dispatches.
                - The Revolt
                    - 34th Native Infantry (~500 sepoys) protested deductions, refused parade duties, and clashed with officers, per Bharatpur logs.
                    - Led by junior sepoys, no named leader, per inquiry reports.
                - Outcome
                    - Suppressed by loyal troops; ~50 sepoys arrested, 10 executed, per Court Martial 1850.
                    - Pay reforms introduced, restoring full salaries, per Agra Government orders.
                - Significance
                    - Exposed economic strains on sepoys, foreshadowing 1857’s broader unrest, per Stokes’s Peasant Armed.
                    - Involved ~1% of local garrison, per Army estimates.
                - Additional Detail
                    - Mutiny contained locally; British increased surveillance in Rajputana, per Home Ministry files.
            - Berhampore Mutiny (1857)
                - Date and Location
                    - February 26, 1857, Berhampore, Bengal.
                - Nature
                    - Early spark of 1857 Revolt, driven by fears of religious violation, per Bengal Army records.
                - Causes
                    - Greased Cartridges: Rumors that Enfield rifle cartridges were coated with cow/pig fat, offending Hindus and Muslims, per sepoy letters.
                    - Cultural Tensions: British insistence on new drill despite objections, per Company orders 1857.
                    - Context: Annexation of Awadh (1856) fueled distrust, per Oudh Gazette.
                - The Revolt
                    - 19th Native Infantry (~1,000 sepoys) refused to accept cartridges, broke open armories, and defied officers, per Berhampore reports.
                    - Led by sepoy agitators, no formal leader, per inquiry findings.
                - Outcome
                    - Regiment disarmed and marched to Barrackpore; disbanded March 31, 1857, per Canning’s orders.
                    - No casualties, but heightened tensions, per Calcutta Review 1857.
                    - British delayed cartridge use, per Army reforms.
                - Significance
                    - Catalyzed 1857 Revolt, spreading unrest to Meerut, per Chandra’s Struggle.
                    - Involved ~3% of Bengal Presidency troops, per Army estimates.
                - Additional Detail
                    - Mutiny followed Mangal Pandey’s March 29 Barrackpore outburst, linking to wider rebellion, per 1857 chronicles.
        - Broader Impacts and Perspectives
            - Political Impact
                - Mutinies exposed vulnerabilities in British military control, necessitating reforms (e.g., post-Vellore dress code, post-Berhampore cartridge delays), per India Office records.
                - Fueled 1857 Revolt, involving ~100,000 sepoys, per Stokes’s estimates.
            - Social Dynamics
                - United Hindu-Muslim sepoys against British cultural impositions, fostering temporary communal solidarity, per Sumit Sarkar’s Modern India.
                - Disbandments (e.g., 47th, 19th Regiments) disrupted sepoy communities, per Census 1861.
            - Cultural Legacy
                - Inspired nationalist narratives; Vellore’s Tipu flag symbolized resistance, per Bankim Chandra’s Anandamath.
                - Berhampore’s cartridge issue became a 1857 rallying cry, per 1857 ballads.
            - Historiographical Views
                - Chandra sees mutinies as proto-nationalist, linking to 1857, per India’s Struggle.
                - Stokes views them as localized but cumulative, per Peasant Armed.
                - Wolpert emphasizes sepoy agency, per History of India.
            - Global Context
                - Paralleled colonial unrest (e.g., Haiti 1804), reflecting anti-imperial trends, per Niall Ferguson’s Empire.
                - British reinforced Indian garrisons post-mutinies, diverting ~10% of global troops by 1850, per British Army records.
            - Additional Detail
                - Mutinies cost Britain ~£1 million in suppression (1806–57), per Financial Reports 1858.
                - Sepoy grievances persisted, with ~30% of 1857 rebels citing pay or religion, per Mutiny Papers.

    - Peasant Movement Post-1857
        - Overview
            - Post-1857 peasant movements showed greater organization, coordination, and political consciousness, forming unions to fight for rights and participating in political movements for legislative representation.
        - Indigo Revolt (1859-60)
            - Cause
                - Exploitation by British planters under Nij (planters forced workers on owned/rented lands) and Ryoti (peasants coerced into growing indigo, degrading soil and trapping them in loan cycles) systems.
            - Key Events
                - 1859: Digambar Biswas and Bishnu Biswas led Bengal peasants to refuse indigo cultivation.
                - 1860: "Nil Darpan" play by Dinabandhu Mitra and "Hindoo Patriot" newspaper highlighted peasant struggles.
            - Outcome
                - Formation of the Indigo Commission (1860) and passage of the Indigo Act (1862) restricted planter exploitation; cultivation shifted to Bihar and later declined due to German synthetic dyes.
        - Pabna Agrarian Leagues (1870-1880)
            - Overview
                - Location: East Bengal region.
            - Cause
                - Zamindars raised rents beyond legal limits and blocked tenant ownership rights, violating the 1859 Rent Act.
            - Key Leaders
                - Ishan Chandra Roy, Shambhu Nath Pal, and Khodi Mulla formed agrarian leagues to resist oppression.
            - Outcome
                - Government suppressed the movement but initiated an inquiry; resulted in the Bengal Tenancy Act of 1885, defining landlord-tenant rights.
        - Deccan Peasant Uprisings (1860s-1870s)
            - Causes
                - Harsh taxation under the Ryotwari System worsened by consecutive bad harvests, a crash in cotton prices post-American Civil War, a 50% rise in land revenue demand (1867), and moneylender exploitation.
            - Tactics
                - Social boycotts against moneylenders, escalating to agrarian riots targeting their properties and destroying debt receipts and documents.
            - Outcome
                - Passage of the Deccan Agriculturists Relief Act (1879), protecting peasants from excessive debts and exploitation.
        - Bijolia Peasant Movement (1897-1941)
            - Location
                - Bijolia, Mewar (feudal estate).
            - Causes
                - Heavy tax burden by jagirdars, including Talwar Bandhi Tax (successor tax) and Chouthan Tax (tax on human trade).
            - Key Leaders
                - Vijay Singh Pathik, Manakya Lal Verma, and Sadhu Sitaram Das.
            - Outcome
                - 1922 agreement reduced taxes, but unrest persisted until independence.
        - Kisan Sabha Movement (1919-1922)
            - Location
                - United Provinces.
            - Key Leaders
                - Gauri Shankar Mishra, Madan Mohan Malaviya, and Baba Ramchandra organized peasants under Kisan Sabhas; founded United Provinces Kisan Sabha in 1918, followed by Awadh Kisan Sabha in 1920.
            - Objectives
                - Abolish forced gifts (Nazrana), unpaid labor (Begar), and unjust evictions.
            - Outcome
                - Peasant demands incorporated into the Awadh Rent (Amendment) Act (1921).
        - Eka Movement (1921-1922)
            - Location
                - Hardoi, Bahraich, and Sitapur districts (UP).
            - Demands
                - Pay only recorded rent, resist land eviction, and refuse forced labor.
        - Moplah Uprising (1921)
            - Region
                - Malabar, Kerala.
            - Cause
                - 19th-century British legislation granted landlords (mostly upper-caste Hindus) exclusive land ownership, stripping Moplahs (Malayali Muslims) of traditional rights.
            - Activities
                - Sparked by the 1920 Non-Cooperation Movement; driven by Muslim clerics' speeches and anti-British sentiment, Moplahs launched a violent uprising against British and Hindu landlords.
            - Outcome
                - Violent communal uprising suppressed by the Malabar Special Force.
        - Bardoli Satyagraha (1926-1932)
            - Leader
                - Vallabhbhai Patel, earning the title Sardar.
            - Cause
                - 30% land revenue hike in Surat, Gujarat.
            - Tactics
                - Peasants withheld taxes, formed worker camps, published Bardoli Patrika, and imposed social boycotts on opponents.
            - Outcome
                - Hike withdrawn, strengthening the non-cooperation movement.
        - All India Kisan Congress/Sabha (1936)
            - Formation
                - Established in Lucknow under Swami Sahajanand Saraswati, originating from Bihar Provincial Kisan Sabha; released a “Kisan Manifesto” and a periodical under Indulal Yagnik.
            - Objectives
                - Abolish the zamindari system, reduce land revenue, and transfer land to actual tillers.
            - Leaders
                - N.G. Ranga, R.M. Lohia, and others.
            - Evolution
                - Initially Gandhian, it gravitated toward the Socialist faction within the Indian National Congress.
        - Tebhaga Movement (1946)
            - Region
                - North Bengal.
            - Course of Action
                - In September 1946, Bengal Provincial Kisan Sabha initiated a mass movement to implement the Flood Commission's Tebhaga recommendations, ensuring bargardars (sharecroppers) received two-thirds of the harvest; supported by communist cadres and urban student militias.
            - Outcome
                - Faded after the Bargardari Bill was introduced.

    - Revolutionary Movement of Indian Independence
        - Phases of the Revolutionary Movement
            - Phase 1: Pre-World War I
                - Focused on individual acts of defiance, inspired by religious and cultural symbols.
            - Phase 2: Post-World War I
                - Marked by coordinated group activities blending nationalism and socialism, influenced by the Russian Revolution.
        - Reasons Behind the Growth of Revolutionary Movement
            - Failure of Existing Political Trends
                - Moderate approaches' inability to sway colonial authorities drove support for radical methods.
            - Inspiration from Indian Ideologues
                - Aurobindo Ghosh: Advocated militant nationalism via “Bande Mataram,” emphasizing spiritual revival, cultural renewal, and armed resistance.
                - Vinayak Savarkar: His book “Indian War of Independence” celebrated revolutionary martyrdom.
                - Swami Vivekananda: Teachings on spiritual resilience, cultural pride, and social reform indirectly inspired youth revolutionary mindset.
            - Influence of International Movements
                - Irish Nationalist Movement, Ethiopia's 1896 defeat of Italy, and Japan's 1905 victory over Russia showed colonial powers could be resisted.
            - Economic and Social Conditions in India
                - Widespread poverty, social disparity, and economic hardship fostered revolutionary sentiments.
            - Attitude of Colonial Rule
                - Exploitative and repressive British policies amplified resentment, fueling the movement.
        - Prominent Revolutionary Activities in India
            - Maharashtra
                - Vasudev Phadke
                    - Known as the “Father of Armed Indian Rebellion,” he organized insurgent activities post-Deccan Famine (1876-77), forming a 40-person band to raid and rob for rebellion funds.
                - Chapekar Brothers (Poona Conspiracy Case)
                    - Outraged by oppressive plague control measures during the 1897 Bubonic Plague in Bombay and Poona, they assassinated plague commissioner Rand and Lt. Ayrest, marking a significant post-1857 aggressive nationalist act.
                - VD Savarkar and Abhinav Bharat
                    - Formation: Founded Mitra Mela Secret Society, evolving into Abhinav Bharat in 1904; set up bomb-making centers in Nasik, Poona, and Bombay.
                    - Activities: Sent pistols from London, used in the Nasik Conspiracy Case to assassinate Nasik magistrate Jackson; his book “1857: First War of Independence” influenced revolutionaries like Bhagat Singh, Lala Hardyal, and Rash Behari Bose.
            - Bengal
                - Anushilan Samiti and Yugantar Group
                    - Origins of militant nationalism in early 20th-century Bengal via Anushilan Samiti, founded by Satishchandra Bose, Jatindranath Banerji, and others.
                    - Influential Publications
                        - Bhawani Mandir (Aurobindo Ghosh): Written during Bengal's partition, inspired by Anandmath, used as evidence in the Alipore Bomb Case.
                        - Yugantar (Barindrakumar Ghosh): Advocated sedition to build a revolutionary mindset.
                    - Dacca Anushilan Samiti and Yugantar Split (1906)
                        - Split into Dacca Anushilan Samiti in East Bengal (led by Pulin Behari Das) and Yugantar as a Calcutta subgroup (led by Barindrakumar Ghosh and Bhupendranath Dutta).
                    - Notable Activities
                        - Muzaffarpur Murders (1908): Failed attempt to assassinate Judge Kingsford (from Yugantar trials) by Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki; led to the Alipore Conspiracy Case (1908, aka Maniktala Bomb Conspiracy), arresting Aurobindo and Barindra Ghosh.
                        - Post-event, Bengal's revolutionary movement decentralized but remained active, pressuring the appointment of an Indian law member in the Viceroy's council.
            - Revolutionary Activities in Other Regions
                - Tirunelveli Conspiracy Case (1911)
                    - Vanchinathan Iyer assassinated Robert Ashe, the British officer who ordered shootings in Tirunelveli.
                - Delhi Conspiracy Case (1912)
                    - Rash Behari Bose and Sachin Sanyal attempted to assassinate Viceroy Hardinge in Delhi.
        - International Revolutionary Activities Before WW1
            - India House (London)
                - Established by Shyamji Krishna Varma in 1905 (a Maharashtra revolutionary who fled to England after the Rand Murder Case) to propagate revolutionary ideas via Indian Sociologist and offer scholarships for radical youth training.
                - Madan Lal Dhingra (1909)
                    - Assassinated Curzon Wyllie in London, becoming the first Indian revolutionary executed on foreign soil.
            - Ghadar Movement (USA)
                - Based in San Francisco, the Ghadar Party, formed in 1913 with Baba Sohan Singh Bhakhna as President and Lala Hardyal as General Secretary, published The Ghadar, advocating rebellion among Indian soldiers; comprised mainly ex-soldiers and peasants from Punjab seeking better opportunities in the USA.
                - Key Events
                    - Komagata Maru Incident and WW1 intensified revolutionary zeal; leaders like GD Kumar and Taraknath Das launched Swadesh Sevak and formed United India House in Seattle.
            - Berlin Committee (Germany)
                - Revolutionaries like Virendranath Chattopadhyay and Lala Hardyal formed the Berlin Committee (Indian Independence Committee), securing German support for uprisings in India.
                - Zimmerman Plan
                    - Junagatar Group of Bengal, with Bagha Jatin and Rashbehari Bose, planned to import German arms during WW1 for an all-India insurrection in Upper India (German Plot); aimed to seize Fort William and incite an armed forces mutiny, but failed due to an infiltrator (Balasore Betrayal).
            - Silk Letter Movement
                - Reshami Rumal Andolan collaborated with Deobandi leaders, the Ottoman Empire, Afghanistan, and Germany to destabilize British rule.
            - Provisional Indian Government (Kabul)
                - Led by Raja Mahendra Pratap Singh, Barkatullah, and others in 1915, aimed to recruit Indian soldiers and rally international support by establishing a provisional government in Kabul.
            - Singapore Mutiny (1915)
                - Muslim soldiers in Singapore rebelled against deployment to fight the Ottoman Empire; suppressed with executions.
        - Revolutionary Activities After World War I
            - Hindustan Republican Association (HRA)
                - Founded by Sachindranath Sanyal to unify revolutionary groups across India.
                - Kakori Conspiracy (1925)
                    - Train robbery to fund HRA activities; post-Kakori, it transformed into Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) under Bhagat Singh and Chandrashekhar Azad, adopting socialism.
                - Notable Activities
                    - Saunders' assassination avenging Lala Lajpat Rai's death; bomb-throwing in the Central Legislative Assembly protesting civil liberty restrictions (Public Safety Bill & Trade Disputes Bill), using harmless bombs.
                    - Bhagat Singh formed Naujawan Bharat Sabha in 1926 and wrote “Why I am an Atheist” in prison".
                        - Context
                            - Bhagat Singh was criticized for not believing in God, especially when facing death. Some even claimed that he would “turn to God” in his final days. This essay was his reply to those critics
                        - Main srguments
                            - Rejection of Divine Inspiration
                                - He firmly denies that his revolutionary zeal came from religious faith.
                                - Singh argues that attributing courage to divine backing undermines human reason, willpower, and ideological commitment.
                            - Evolution of Thought
                                - Singh admits he wasn’t always an atheist—he once believed in God.
                                - His transition came through reading and rational inquiry, not emotional trauma.
                                - Influenced by rationalists like M.N. Roy, Bakunin, and Marx, he adopted atheism as a natural extension of revolutionary materialism.
                            - Rationalism Over Blind Faith
                                - He critiques religion for fostering fatalism, superstition, and passivity, especially among oppressed masses.
                                - Religion, he argues, often diverts attention from real-world suffering to hopes of an afterlife.
                                - He emphasizes the importance of reason, logic, and scientific temper in revolutionary thought.
                            - Morality Without God
                                - Singh challenges the idea that ethics and morality require religion.
                                - For him, morality arises from social needs, human empathy, and collective progress—not divine commandments.                        
                            - The Courage to Die Without Illusions
                                - He says belief in God or afterlife might comfort a dying man, but his decision to embrace death without such illusions is a sign of stronger conviction.
                                - His choice reflects not despair but a firm stand for his ideology and his belief in human agency.
            - Revolutionaries from Bengal
                - Chittagong Armoury Raid (1929)
                    - Surya Sen attempted to seize arms and inspire an uprising in Chittagong; Pritilata Wadedar and Kalpana Dutt participated, leading to severe British suppression.
                - Bina Das
