        - Q45: Which of the following pairs are correctly matched?
            - List:
                1. AD 1767-69: First Anglo-Maratha War
                2. AD 1790-92: Third Mysore War
                3. AD 1824-26: First Anglo-Burmese War
                4. AD 1845-46: Second Sikh War
            - Options:
                (a) 2 and 4
                (b) 3 and 4
                (c) 1 and 2
                (d) 2 and 3
            - Answer:
                (d) 2 and 3
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct pairs:
                        - 2. Third Mysore War (1790-1792): Fought under Lord Cornwallis against Tipu Sultan, ended with the Treaty of Seringapatam, ceding half of Mysore’s territory.
                        - 3. First Anglo-Burmese War (1824-1826): Led by Lord Amherst, resulted in British annexation of Assam, Manipur, and Arakan via the Treaty of Yandabo (Q25).
                    - Incorrect pairs:
                        - 1. First Anglo-Maratha War: Occurred in 1775-1782, not 1767-1769, under Warren Hastings, ended with the Treaty of Salbai (Q25).
                        - 4. Second Sikh War: Took place in 1848-1849, not 1845-1846, under Lord Dalhousie, leading to Punjab’s annexation (Q26).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 2 and 4:
                        - Incorrect—Pair 4 is wrong (Second Sikh War was 1848-1849).
                    - (b) 3 and 4:
                        - Incorrect—Pair 4 is wrong (Second Sikh War was 1848-1849).
                    - (c) 1 and 2:
                        - Incorrect—Pair 1 is wrong (First Anglo-Maratha War was 1775-1782).
                - Other Relevant Colonial Wars:
                    - Second Anglo-Maratha War (1803-1805):
                        - Under Wellesley, expanded British control, post-First Maratha War.
                    - Fourth Anglo-Mysore War (1799):
                        - Defeated Tipu Sultan, followed Third Mysore War (Q21).
                    - First Sikh War (1845-1846):
                        - Preceded Second Sikh War, weakened Sikh power.
                - Significance of These Wars:
                    - The Third Mysore War curtailed Tipu’s power, strengthening British dominance in South India.
                    - The First Anglo-Burmese War expanded British territory in Northeast India, shaping regional geopolitics.
                    - Correct dating is crucial for UPSC, as misattributions (e.g., Maratha, Sikh wars) are common traps.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Cornwallis:
                        - Led Third Mysore War, secured British gains.
                    - Lord Amherst:
                        - Governor-General during First Anglo-Burmese War.
                    - Warren Hastings:
                        - Oversaw First Anglo-Maratha War, relevant to pair 1’s correction.
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Led Second Sikh War, relevant to pair 4’s correction.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on British expansion (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Anglo-Mysore Wars), colonial wars (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Sikh Wars), or frontier policies (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Burmese conflicts).
                    - Reflects Britain’s phased conquest of India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The Third Mysore War (1790-1792) and First Anglo-Burmese War (1824-1826) are correctly matched, highlighting British territorial expansion in South and Northeast India.
        - Q46: Which of the following pairs is not correctly matched?
            - Options:
                (a) Pitt’s India Act—Warren Hastings
                (b) Doctrine of Lapse—Dalhousie
                (c) Vernacular Press Act—Curzon
                (d) Ilbert Bill—Ripon
            - Answer:
                (c) Vernacular Press Act—Curzon
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct pairs:
                        - (a) Pitt’s India Act (1784): Enacted during Warren Hastings’ tenure, established a Board of Control to regulate the East India Company (Q35).
                        - (b) Doctrine of Lapse (1848-1856): Introduced by Lord Dalhousie, used to annex princely states like Satara and Jhansi (Q26).
                        - (d) Ilbert Bill (1883): Proposed by Lord Ripon, aimed at judicial equality for Indians and Europeans, sparking controversy (Q43).
                    - Incorrect pair:
                        - (c) Vernacular Press Act (1878): Enacted under Lord Lytton, not Curzon, to curb the Indian press; Curzon (1899-1905) was associated with the Bengal Partition (1905).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Correct—Pitt’s India Act aligned with Hastings’ governorship, enhancing British oversight.
                    - (b) Correct—Dalhousie’s Doctrine of Lapse was a key annexation policy.
                    - (d) Correct—Ripon’s Ilbert Bill was a landmark liberal reform.
                - Other Relevant Colonial Policies:
                    - Regulating Act, 1773:
                        - Preceded Pitt’s India Act, also under Hastings (Q35).
                    - Bengal Tenancy Act, 1885:
                        - Ripon’s reform, complemented Ilbert Bill’s liberal agenda.
                    - Universities Act, 1904:
                        - Curzon’s actual policy, contrasting with Vernacular Press Act.
                - Significance of the Vernacular Press Act:
                    - The Act, wrongly attributed to Curzon, suppressed nationalist newspapers under Lytton, fueling Indian discontent and early nationalism.
                    - Its misattribution to Curzon is a UPSC trap, testing precise knowledge of Viceroys’ tenures.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Warren Hastings:
                        - First Governor-General, governed under Pitt’s India Act.
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Architect of Doctrine of Lapse, expanded British India.
                    - Lord Lytton:
                        - Enacted Vernacular Press Act, not Curzon.
                    - Lord Ripon:
                        - Proposed Ilbert Bill, championed liberal reforms.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial governance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Viceroys), press freedom (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Vernacular Press Act), or annexation policies (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Doctrine of Lapse).
                    - Reflects the diverse strategies of British control, from censorship to territorial expansion.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The Vernacular Press Act, incorrectly paired with Curzon, was Lytton’s 1878 policy, underscoring the need for accurate Viceroy-policy associations in colonial history.
        - Q47: Consider the following Viceroys of India during the British rule:
            - List:
                1. Lord Curzon
                2. Lord Chelmsford
                3. Lord Hardinge
                4. Lord Irwin
            - The correct chronological order of their tenure is:
            - Options:
                (a) 1, 3, 2, 4
                (b) 2, 4, 1, 3
                (c) 1, 4, 2, 3
                (d) 2, 3, 1, 4
            - Answer:
                (a) 1, 3, 2, 4
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct chronological order:
                        - 1. Lord Curzon (1899-1905): Known for Bengal Partition and administrative reforms.
                        - 3. Lord Hardinge (1910-1916): Oversaw Delhi Durbar (1911) and shifted capital to Delhi.
                        - 2. Lord Chelmsford (1916-1921): Implemented Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (1919).
                        - 4. Lord Irwin (1926-1931): Negotiated Gandhi-Irwin Pact (1931), faced Civil Disobedience.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) 2, 4, 1, 3:
                        - Incorrect—Chelmsford (1916-1921) and Irwin (1926-1931) postdate Curzon (1899-1905).
                    - (c) 1, 4, 2, 3:
                        - Incorrect—Irwin (1926-1931) follows Chelmsford (1916-1921), not precedes.
                    - (d) 2, 3, 1, 4:
                        - Incorrect—Chelmsford (1916-1921) follows Hardinge (1910-1916), not precedes.
                - Other Relevant Viceroys:
                    - Lord Minto (1905-1910):
                        - Preceded Hardinge, introduced Morley-Minto Reforms.
                    - Lord Willingdon (1931-1936):
                        - Followed Irwin, oversaw Government of India Act, 1935.
                    - Lord Linlithgow (1936-1943):
                        - Post-Irwin, managed early WWII challenges.
                - Significance of Their Tenures:
                    - Curzon’s partition fueled nationalist unrest, shaping early 20th-century politics.
                    - Hardinge’s capital shift and Delhi Durbar symbolized imperial consolidation.
                    - Chelmsford’s reforms introduced limited self-governance, a precursor to later constitutional changes.
                    - Irwin’s pact with Gandhi marked a turning point in British-Indian negotiations.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Curzon:
                        - Partitioned Bengal, centralized administration.
                    - Lord Hardinge:
                        - Shifted capital to Delhi, faced nationalist protests.
                    - Lord Chelmsford:
                        - Co-authored 1919 reforms with Montagu.
                    - Lord Irwin:
                        - Engaged with Gandhi, navigated Civil Disobedience.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Viceroys (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Curzon), constitutional reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Montagu-Chelmsford), or nationalist movements (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Gandhi-Irwin Pact).
                    - Reflects the evolving British response to Indian nationalism, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The correct chronological order (Curzon, Hardinge, Chelmsford, Irwin) traces the progression of British Viceroys, each shaping colonial governance and nationalist responses.
        - Q48: Consider the following princely States of the British rule in India:
            - List:
                1. Jhansi
                2. Sambalpur
                3. Satara
            - The correct chronological order in which they were annexed by the British is:
            - Options:
                (a) 1, 2, 3
                (b) 1, 3, 2
                (c) 3, 2, 1
                (d) 3, 1, 2
            - Answer:
                (c) 3, 2, 1
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct chronological order (under Lord Dalhousie’s Doctrine of Lapse):
                        - 3. Satara (1848): Annexed after the death of Raja Appa Sahib, lacking a natural heir.
                        - 2. Sambalpur (1849): Annexed following the death of Raja Narayan Singh, without a direct heir.
                        - 1. Jhansi (1853): Annexed after Raja Gangadhar Rao’s death, rejecting the adopted heir Damodar Rao (Q26).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1, 2, 3:
                        - Incorrect—Jhansi (1853) was annexed after Satara (1848) and Sambalpur (1849).
                    - (b) 1, 3, 2:
                        - Incorrect—Jhansi (1853) follows Satara (1848) and Sambalpur (1849).
                    - (d) 3, 1, 2:
                        - Incorrect—Sambalpur (1849) precedes Jhansi (1853).
                - Other Relevant Annexations under Doctrine of Lapse:
                    - Nagpur (1854):
                        - Annexed post-Jhansi, under Dalhousie’s policy.
                    - Jaitpur (1849):
                        - Annexed alongside Sambalpur, due to lack of heirs.
                    - Awadh (1856):
                        - Annexed on grounds of misgovernance, not Doctrine of Lapse.
                - Significance of These Annexations:
                    - Dalhousie’s Doctrine of Lapse expanded British territory but provoked resentment among Indian rulers, contributing to the 1857 Revolt, notably in Jhansi under Rani Lakshmibai.
                    - The annexations centralized British control, reducing princely autonomy and fueling nationalist sentiments.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Architect of Doctrine of Lapse, drove annexations.
                    - Rani Lakshmibai:
                        - Jhansi’s queen, led 1857 Revolt against annexation.
                    - Appa Sahib:
                        - Satara’s ruler, whose death triggered annexation.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Doctrine of Lapse (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Dalhousie), 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Jhansi), or princely states (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on annexations).
                    - Reflects British territorial consolidation, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The correct annexation order (Satara, Sambalpur, Jhansi) under Dalhousie’s Doctrine of Lapse highlights the aggressive British expansion that fueled the 1857 Revolt.
        - Q49: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. In the Third Battle of Panipat, Ahmed Shah Abdali defeated Ibrahim Lodi
                2. Tipu Sultan was killed in the Third Anglo-Mysore War
                3. Mir Jafar entered in a conspiracy with the English for the defeat of Nawab Siraj-ud-Daulah in the Battle of Plassey
            - Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1, 2 and 3
                (b) 3 only
                (c) 2 and 3
                (d) None
            - Answer:
                (b) 3 only
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statement:
                        - 3. Mir Jafar, a commander under Nawab Siraj-ud-Daulah, conspired with the English (Robert Clive) to betray the Nawab at the Battle of Plassey (1757), securing British control over Bengal.
                    - Incorrect statements:
                        - 1. Third Battle of Panipat (1761): Ahmed Shah Abdali defeated the Marathas under Sadashivrao Bhau, not Ibrahim Lodi, who was defeated by Babur in the First Battle of Panipat (1526).
                        - 2. Tipu Sultan was killed in the Fourth Anglo-Mysore War (1799), not the Third (1790-1792), during the Siege of Seringapatam under Lord Wellesley (Q21).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1, 2 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Statements 1 and 2 are false.
                    - (c) 2 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 2 is false; only 3 is correct.
                    - (d) None:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 3 is correct.
                - Other Relevant Pre-Colonial and Colonial Conflicts:
                    - First Battle of Panipat (1526):
                        - Babur defeated Ibrahim Lodi, relevant to statement 1’s correction.
                    - Battle of Buxar (1764):
                        - Consolidated British power post-Plassey, involving Mir Jafar’s successors.
                    - Second Anglo-Mysore War (1780-1784):
                        - Preceded Third Mysore War, involved Tipu’s resistance.
                - Significance of These Events:
                    - The Plassey conspiracy (statement 3) marked the beginning of British political dominance in India, enabling the East India Company’s expansion.
                    - Misattributions in statements 1 and 2 (Panipat, Mysore) are UPSC traps, testing precise historical knowledge.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Mir Jafar:
                        - Betrayed Siraj-ud-Daulah, installed as Nawab post-Plassey.
                    - Ahmed Shah Abdali:
                        - Afghan leader, won Third Panipat, not against Lodi.
                    - Tipu Sultan:
                        - Killed in Fourth Mysore War, not Third.
                    - Robert Clive:
                        - Led English at Plassey, orchestrated Mir Jafar’s conspiracy.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on early British expansion (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Plassey), Anglo-Mysore Wars (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Tipu), or pre-colonial battles (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Panipat).
                    - Reflects the transition from Mughal to British dominance, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Only the statement about Mir Jafar’s conspiracy at Plassey is correct, underscoring the pivotal role of betrayal in establishing British control in India.

        - Q50: Which one of the following places did Kunwar Singh, a prominent leader of the Revolt of 1857 belong to?
            - Options:
                (a) Bihar
                (b) Madhya Pradesh
                (c) Rajasthan
                (d) Uttar Pradesh
            - Answer:
                (a) Bihar
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Kunwar Singh, a Rajput zamindar from Jagdishpur (Arrah district, Bihar), was a prominent leader of the 1857 Revolt, leading resistance against the British in Bihar at the age of nearly 80.
                    - He mobilized local forces, captured Arrah, and coordinated with other rebels like Nana Saheb, despite facing British counterattacks, until his death in 1858 from wounds sustained in battle.
                    - His leadership in Bihar was crucial in extending the revolt beyond the Gangetic plain, challenging British control in eastern India.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) Madhya Pradesh:
                        - Incorrect—No historical record links Kunwar Singh to Madhya Pradesh; the region saw leaders like Rani Lakshmibai (Jhansi).
                    - (c) Rajasthan:
                        - Incorrect—Rajasthan had leaders like Tantia Tope, but Kunwar Singh was from Bihar.
                    - (d) Uttar Pradesh:
                        - Incorrect—U.P. was a major revolt center (e.g., Lucknow, Kanpur), but Kunwar Singh’s base was Jagdishpur, Bihar.
                - Other Relevant 1857 Revolt Leaders:
                    - Rani Lakshmibai:
                        - Led the revolt in Jhansi, Central India (Q48).
                    - Nana Saheb:
                        - Rebel leader in Kanpur, coordinated with Kunwar Singh.
                    - Bahadur Shah Zafar:
                        - Symbolic Mughal leader in Delhi, linked to Bihar rebels.
                - Significance of Kunwar Singh’s Role:
                    - Kunwar Singh’s leadership in Bihar demonstrated the revolt’s widespread appeal, uniting zamindars, peasants, and sepoys against British policies like land revenue and annexation.
                    - His guerrilla tactics and regional influence prolonged resistance, contributing to the revolt’s legacy as a proto-nationalist uprising.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Kunwar Singh:
                        - Zamindar and rebel leader, symbolized Bihar’s resistance.
                    - William Tayler:
                        - British Commissioner in Patna, opposed Kunwar Singh’s forces.
                    - Lord Canning:
                        - Viceroy during 1857, managed British response to Bihar rebellion.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on rebel leaders), regional resistance (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Bihar), or colonial policies (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on zamindari discontent).
                    - Reflects the diverse leadership of the 1857 Revolt, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Kunwar Singh, from Bihar, was a key 1857 Revolt leader, whose Jagdishpur-based resistance highlighted the uprising’s regional diversity and anti-colonial fervor.
        - Q51: Which one of the following territories was not affected by the Revolt of 1857?
            - Options:
                (a) Jhansi
                (b) Chittor
                (c) Jagdishpur
                (d) Lucknow
            - Answer:
                (b) Chittor
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Revolt of 1857 primarily affected North and Central India, including Bengal, Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, and parts of Madhya Pradesh, driven by sepoys, zamindars, and princes.
                    - Affected territories:
                        - (a) Jhansi: Led by Rani Lakshmibai, a major revolt center, annexed by Dalhousie (Q48).
                        - (c) Jagdishpur: Kunwar Singh’s base in Bihar, a focal point of rebellion (Q50).
                        - (d) Lucknow: Key center under Begum Hazrat Mahal, saw prolonged siege by British forces.
                    - Unaffected territory:
                        - (b) Chittor (Rajasthan): No significant revolt activity; Rajasthan saw some unrest (e.g., Kota, Ajmer), but Chittor remained largely unaffected.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Jhansi:
                        - Incorrect—Jhansi was a major revolt hub under Rani Lakshmibai.
                    - (c) Jagdishpur:
                        - Incorrect—Jagdishpur was central to Kunwar Singh’s rebellion.
                    - (d) Lucknow:
                        - Incorrect—Lucknow was a key revolt site, with intense fighting.
                - Other Relevant Unaffected Regions:
                    - South India:
                        - Madras Presidency, Hyderabad, and Travancore remained largely loyal to the British.
                    - Punjab:
                        - Controlled by British allies like Sikh chiefs, suppressed revolt spread.
                    - Bombay Presidency:
                        - Limited unrest, quickly contained by British forces.
                - Significance of Chittor’s Non-Involvement:
                    - Chittor’s lack of revolt activity reflects the uneven spread of the 1857 uprising, limited by regional loyalties, British alliances, and communication barriers.
                    - It underscores the revolt’s concentration in areas affected by British annexation and revenue policies, sparing regions like parts of Rajasthan.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Rani Lakshmibai:
                        - Jhansi’s leader, epitomized revolt’s intensity (Q48).
                    - Kunwar Singh:
                        - Jagdishpur’s rebel, drove Bihar’s uprising (Q50).
                    - Begum Hazrat Mahal:
                        - Led Lucknow’s resistance, symbolizing Awadh’s defiance.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on affected areas), regional dynamics (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Rajasthan), or British policies (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on annexation impacts).
                    - Reflects the geographic scope of the 1857 Revolt, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Chittor was unaffected by the 1857 Revolt, unlike Jhansi, Jagdishpur, and Lucknow, highlighting the uprising’s regional limitations and British control in Rajasthan.
        - Q52: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar founded the Bethune School at Calcutta with the main aim of encouraging education for women
                2. Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay was the first graduate of the Calcutta University
                3. Keshav Chandra Sen’s campaign against Sati led to the enactment of a law to ban Sati by the Governor General
            - Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1 only
                (b) 1 and 2
                (c) 2 and 3
                (d) 1, 2 and 3
            - Answer:
                (a) 1 only
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statement:
                        - 1. Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar, a key Bengal Renaissance reformer, supported women’s education and was instrumental in establishing the Bethune School (1849) in Calcutta, aimed at promoting female education, though founded by John Bethune.
                    - Incorrect statements:
                        - 2. Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay was not the first Calcutta University graduate; Joddu Nath Bose and Bankim were among the first graduates in 1858, but no single “first” is officially recognized.
                        - 3. Keshav Chandra Sen campaigned for social reforms, but the Sati ban (1829) was enacted under Lord Bentinck, influenced by Raja Rammohan Roy, not Sen, who was active later (1860s-1880s).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) 1 and 2:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 2 is false; no single first graduate is confirmed.
                    - (c) 2 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Both statements 2 and 3 are false.
                    - (d) 1, 2 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Only statement 1 is correct.
                - Other Relevant Social Reform Initiatives:
                    - Widow Remarriage Act, 1856:
                        - Vidyasagar’s advocacy led to its passage, complementing Bethune School.
                    - Brahmo Samaj Reforms:
                        - Keshav Chandra Sen’s women’s education campaigns, though not Sati-related (Q31).
                    - Anandamath (1882):
                        - Bankim’s novel, reflecting his nationalist contributions, not university graduation.
                - Significance of Bethune School:
                    - The Bethune School was a pioneering institution for women’s education in India, fostering gender equality and influencing later reforms like the Widow Remarriage Act.
                    - Misattributions in statements 2 and 3 highlight UPSC’s focus on precise reformer contributions and timelines.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar:
                        - Bengal reformer, championed Bethune School and widow remarriage.
                    - John Bethune:
                        - Founded Bethune School, collaborated with Vidyasagar.
                    - Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Led Sati ban campaign, relevant to statement 3’s correction.
                    - Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay:
                        - Early Calcutta graduate, nationalist author, not the “first.”
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Vidyasagar), social reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Sati), or education (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on women’s education).
                    - Reflects the 19th-century reformist zeal in colonial India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Only Vidyasagar’s role in Bethune School is correct, underscoring his contribution to women’s education and the need for accurate reformer-timeline associations.
        - Q53: Who among the following repealed the Vernacular Press Act?
            - Options:
                (a) Lord Dufferin
                (b) Lord Ripon
                (c) Lord Curzon
                (d) Lord Hardinge
            - Answer:
                (b) Lord Ripon
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Vernacular Press Act, 1878, enacted under Lord Lytton, imposed strict censorship on Indian-language newspapers to curb nationalist sentiments (Q46).
                    - Lord Ripon, a liberal Viceroy (1880-1884), repealed the Act in 1882, responding to widespread Indian protests and aligning with his progressive policies, such as the Ilbert Bill (Q43).
                    - The repeal restored press freedom, boosting nationalist journalism and organizations like the Indian Association (Q33).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Lord Dufferin (1884-1888):
                        - Incorrect—Dufferin focused on administrative stability, not press reform.
                    - (c) Lord Curzon (1899-1905):
                        - Incorrect—Curzon tightened press controls, e.g., via the Universities Act, 1904.
                    - (d) Lord Hardinge (1910-1916):
                        - Incorrect—Hardinge dealt with later nationalist unrest, not the 1878 Act.
                - Other Relevant Press-Related Policies:
                    - Press Act, 1910:
                        - Under Hardinge, reintroduced press restrictions, contrasting Ripon’s repeal.
                    - Indian Press (Emergency Powers) Act, 1931:
                        - Later censorship measure, reflecting cyclical press controls.
                    - Amrita Bazar Patrika:
                        - Nationalist newspaper, benefited from Ripon’s repeal.
                - Significance of the Repeal:
                    - Ripon’s repeal of the Vernacular Press Act empowered Indian newspapers, fostering nationalist discourse and early political organizations like the Indian National Congress (1885).
                    - It marked a rare liberal concession in colonial policy, though limited by subsequent restrictions.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Ripon:
                        - Liberal Viceroy, repealed Act, championed reforms.
                    - Lord Lytton:
                        - Enacted the Act, suppressed press freedom (Q46).
                    - Surendranath Banerjea:
                        - Nationalist leader, leveraged press freedom post-repeal (Q33).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial press policies (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Vernacular Press Act), liberal reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Ripon), or nationalism (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on press freedom).
                    - Reflects the tension between colonial control and Indian agency, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Lord Ripon’s repeal of the Vernacular Press Act in 1882 restored press freedom, boosting nationalist journalism and marking a significant liberal reform.
        - Q54: Which one of the following is the correct statement?
            - Options:
                (a) The modern Kochi was a Dutch colony till India’s independence
                (b) The Dutch defeated the Portuguese and built Fort Williams in the modern Kochi
                (c) The modern Kochi was first a Dutch colony before the Portuguese took over
                (d) The modern Kochi never became a part of a British colony
            - Answer:
                (d) The modern Kochi never became a part of a British colony
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Kochi (Cochin), a key port in Kerala, was a significant European trading hub but remained under the Cochin princely state, allied with European powers.
                    - Correct statement:
                        - (d) Kochi never became a British colony; it was a princely state under British paramountcy, maintaining autonomy while aligned with the British, unlike direct colonies like Bombay.
                    - Incorrect statements:
                        - (a) Kochi was not Dutch until 1947; the Dutch controlled it from 1663 to 1795, after which the British gained influence, but it remained a princely state.
                        - (b) The Dutch did defeat the Portuguese in 1663 but built Fort Cochin, not Fort Williams (a British fort in Calcutta).
                        - (c) Kochi was first a Portuguese base (1503) before the Dutch took over in 1663, not vice versa.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Incorrect—Dutch control ended in 1795; Kochi was a princely state by independence.
                    - (b) Incorrect—Fort Williams is in Calcutta, not Kochi; Dutch built Fort Cochin.
                    - (c) Incorrect—Portuguese preceded Dutch in Kochi, not the reverse.
                - Other Relevant European Influence in Kochi:
                    - Portuguese Fort Emmanuel (1503):
                        - Established Kochi as a Portuguese base, pre-Dutch.
                    - Dutch Fort Cochin (1663):
                        - Replaced Portuguese control, later ceded to British influence.
                    - Travancore Alliance:
                        - Neighboring princely state, similarly autonomous under British paramountcy.
                - Significance of Kochi’s Status:
                    - Kochi’s status as a non-colonized princely state highlights the complexity of British indirect rule, balancing European influence with local autonomy.
                    - It corrects common misconceptions about European colonial control in South India.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Vasco da Gama:
                        - Portuguese explorer, established Kochi base in 1503.
                    - Pieter van den Broecke:
                        - Dutch merchant, involved in Kochi’s transition (Q39).
                    - Raja Rama Varma:
                        - Cochin ruler, maintained autonomy under British paramountcy.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on European colonialism (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Dutch-Portuguese), princely states (e.g., Q76 [2006] on paramountcy), or South India (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Kochi).
                    - Reflects the layered European-Indian interactions in coastal India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Kochi’s status as a princely state, never a British colony, underscores the nuanced British paramountcy system, distinct from direct colonial rule.
        - Q55: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. Warren Hastings was the first Governor General who established a regular police force in India on the British pattern
                2. A Supreme Court was established at Calcutta by the Regulating Act, 1773
                3. The Indian Penal Code came into effect in the year 1860
            - Which of the statements given above are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1 and 2
                (b) 2 and 3
                (c) 1 and 3
                (d) 1, 2 and 3
            - Answer:
                (b) 2 and 3
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statements:
                        - 2. The Regulating Act, 1773, established a Supreme Court at Calcutta, with Sir Elijah Impey as the first Chief Justice, to administer justice for British subjects (Q35).
                        - 3. The Indian Penal Code, drafted by Thomas Macaulay, was enacted in 1860 and came into effect in 1862, standardizing criminal law in British India.
                    - Incorrect statement:
                        - 1. Warren Hastings reorganized police systems, but a regular British-pattern police force was established later under Lord Cornwallis (1790s); Hastings relied on local thanedars and zamindars.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1 and 2:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is false; only 2 is correct.
                    - (c) 1 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is false; only 3 is correct.
                    - (d) 1, 2 and 3:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is false.
                - Other Relevant Colonial Administrative Reforms:
                    - Cornwallis Code (1793):
                        - Established modern police under Cornwallis, correcting statement 1.
                    - Indian High Courts Act, 1861:
                        - Replaced Supreme Court with High Courts, post-1773.
                    - Code of Criminal Procedure, 1861:
                        - Complemented Indian Penal Code, standardizing legal processes.
                - Significance of These Reforms:
                    - The Supreme Court (1773) centralized British judicial authority, though limited to Calcutta’s Europeans, shaping colonial legal systems.
                    - The Indian Penal Code (1860) unified criminal law, influencing post-independence legal frameworks.
                    - The police reform misattribution to Hastings highlights UPSC’s focus on precise administrative timelines.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Warren Hastings:
                        - First Governor-General, reformed local police, not British-pattern (Q46).
                    - Lord Cornwallis:
                        - Established regular police, relevant to statement 1’s correction.
                    - Thomas Macaulay:
                        - Drafted Indian Penal Code, key to statement 3.
                    - Elijah Impey:
                        - First Chief Justice of Calcutta Supreme Court.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial administration (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Regulating Act), legal reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Penal Code), or police systems (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Cornwallis).
                    - Reflects the British effort to impose standardized governance in India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The Regulating Act’s Supreme Court and the Indian Penal Code’s enactment are correct, but Hastings’ police reform is misattributed, emphasizing Cornwallis’ role in modern policing.
        - Q56: Which one of the following is the correct chronological order of the battles fought in India in the 18th Century?
            - Options:
                (a) Battle of Wandiwash—Battle of Buxar—Battle of Ambur—Battle of Plassey
                (b) Battle of Ambur—Battle of Plassey—Battle of Wandiwash—Battle of Buxar
                (c) Battle of Wandiwash—Battle of Plassey—Battle of Ambur—Battle of Buxar
                (d) Battle of Ambur—Battle of Buxar—Battle of Wandiwash—Battle of Plassey
            - Answer:
                (b) Battle of Ambur—Battle of Plassey—Battle of Wandiwash—Battle of Buxar
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct chronological order:
                        - Battle of Ambur (1749): First Carnatic War, Muzaffar Jung’s victory with French support, marked French influence in Hyderabad.
                        - Battle of Plassey (1757): Robert Clive defeated Siraj-ud-Daulah, established British control in Bengal (Q49).
                        - Battle of Wandiwash (1760): Third Carnatic War, British under Eyre Coote defeated French, ending French dominance in South India.
                        - Battle of Buxar (1764): British under Hector Munro defeated Mughal-Shuja-ud-Daulah-Mir Qasim alliance, consolidated Bengal control.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Wandiwash—Buxar—Ambur—Plassey:
                        - Incorrect—Wandiwash (1760) follows Ambur (1749) and Plassey (1757).
                    - (c) Wandiwash—Plassey—Ambur—Buxar:
                        - Incorrect—Wandiwash (1760) follows Ambur (1749); Ambur precedes Plassey.
                    - (d) Ambur—Buxar—Wandiwash—Plassey:
                        - Incorrect—Buxar (1764) follows Plassey (1757); Plassey precedes Wandiwash.
                - Other Relevant 18th-Century Battles:
                    - Battle of Arcot (1751):
                        - Second Carnatic War, Clive’s victory, preceded Wandiwash.
                    - Battle of Panipat (1761):
                        - Third Panipat, Abdali vs. Marathas, contemporary to Wandiwash (Q49).
                    - Battle of Talikota (1565):
                        - Pre-18th century, included for contrast, not relevant here.
                - Significance of These Battles:
                    - Ambur and Plassey established European (French, British) political influence in India, shifting power from regional rulers.
                    - Wandiwash and Buxar cemented British supremacy over French and Mughal forces, paving the way for British hegemony by the late 18th century.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Robert Clive:
                        - Led Plassey, key to British Bengal conquest (Q49).
                    - Eyre Coote:
                        - Won Wandiwash, defeated French in South India.
                    - Hector Munro:
                        - Led Buxar, secured British control in North India.
                    - Muzaffar Jung:
                        - Hyderabad ruler, allied with French at Ambur.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on British expansion (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Plassey), Carnatic Wars (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Wandiwash), or Mughal decline (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Buxar).
                    - Reflects the 18th-century transition to British dominance, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The correct battle order (Ambur, Plassey, Wandiwash, Buxar) traces the rise of British power in India, defeating regional and European rivals in the 18th century.
        - Q57: Who among the following was a proponent of Fabianism as a movement?
            - Options:
                (a) Annie Besant
                (b) A.O. Hume
                (c) Michael Madhusudan Dutt
                (d) R. Palme Dutt
            - Answer:
                (d) R. Palme Dutt
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Fabianism, a British socialist movement founded in 1884, advocated gradual, reformist socialism through parliamentary means, influencing global leftist thought.
                    - Rajani Palme Dutt (R. Palme Dutt), an Indian-British communist, was a proponent of Fabian-inspired socialism, blending Marxist and reformist ideas in his writings and leadership in the Communist Party of Great Britain.
                    - His works, like “India Today” (1940), critiqued colonial exploitation, aligning Fabian gradualism with anti-colonial activism.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Annie Besant:
                        - Incorrect—Besant was a theosophist and Home Rule League leader, not a Fabian socialist.
                    - (b) A.O. Hume:
                        - Incorrect—Hume founded the Indian National Congress, focused on moderate nationalism, not socialism.
                    - (c) Michael Madhusudan Dutt:
                        - Incorrect—Dutt was a Bengali poet and dramatist, unrelated to Fabianism.
                - Other Relevant Socialist Movements in India:
                    - Congress Socialist Party (1934):
                        - Led by Jayaprakash Narayan, shared Fabian reformist ideals.
                    - Communist Party of India (1925):
                        - R. Palme Dutt’s affiliation, influenced by Fabian tactics.
                    - Swaraj Party (1923):
                        - Moderate nationalism, contrasted with socialist radicalism.
                - Significance of Fabianism in India:
                    - Dutt’s Fabianism influenced Indian leftist thought, bridging British socialism with anti-colonial struggles, shaping early communist and socialist movements.
                    - It provided a reformist alternative to revolutionary socialism, appealing to intellectuals in colonial India.
                - Related Figures:
                    - R. Palme Dutt:
                        - Indian-British communist, applied Fabianism to colonial critique.
                    - George Bernard Shaw:
                        - British Fabian leader, contextual to Dutt’s ideology.
                    - Jawaharlal Nehru:
                        - Influenced by Fabian socialism, shaped Congress’s socialist policies.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Indian socialism (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on leftist movements), colonial nationalism (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Congress), or global ideologies (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on communism).
                    - Reflects the global ideological influences on Indian anti-colonial thought, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - R. Palme Dutt’s advocacy of Fabianism linked socialist reformism to India’s anti-colonial struggle, influencing early leftist movements.

        - Q58: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. The Charter Act, 1853 abolished East India Company monopoly of Indian trade
                2. Under the Government of India Act, 1858 the British Parliament abolished the rule of East India Company and undertook the responsibility of ruling India directly
            - Which of the statement(s) given above is/are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1 only
                (b) 2 only
                (c) Both 1 and 2
                (d) Neither 1 nor 2
            - Answer:
                (b) 2 only
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statement:
                        - 2. The Government of India Act, 1858, abolished the East India Company’s rule post-1857 Revolt, transferring governance to the British Crown, with a Viceroy and Secretary of State for India (Q35).
                    - Incorrect statement:
                        - 1. The Charter Act, 1853, did not abolish the Company’s trade monopoly; this was done by the Charter Act, 1813 (for India trade) and Charter Act, 1833 (for China trade). The 1853 Act extended the Company’s charter and introduced open competition for civil services (Q42).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1 only:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is false; the 1853 Act did not end the trade monopoly.
                    - (c) Both 1 and 2:
                        - Incorrect—Only statement 2 is correct.
                    - (d) Neither 1 nor 2:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 2 is correct.
                - Other Relevant Colonial Legislative Acts:
                    - Charter Act, 1813:
                        - Ended the Company’s India trade monopoly, relevant to statement 1’s correction (Q35).
                    - Charter Act, 1833:
                        - Ended the Company’s China trade monopoly, completed trade liberalization (Q42).
                    - Indian Councils Act, 1861:
                        - Built on 1858 Act, introduced portfolio system (Q36).
                - Significance of the 1858 Act:
                    - The transfer of power to the Crown marked a shift from commercial to imperial rule, centralizing British authority and responding to the 1857 Revolt’s failures.
                    - The misattribution in statement 1 (1853 Act) is a common UPSC trap, testing precise knowledge of Charter Acts’ provisions.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Canning:
                        - First Viceroy under the 1858 Act, managed post-revolt transition.
                    - Charles Wood:
                        - Secretary of State, influenced 1853 and 1858 Acts’ provisions.
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Governor-General before 1858, whose policies sparked revolt.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial governance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on 1858 Act), Charter Acts (e.g., Q76 [2006] on trade reforms), or 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Crown rule).
                    - Reflects the transition from Company to Crown rule, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Only the Government of India Act, 1858, correctly abolished Company rule, while the Charter Act, 1853, did not end trade monopoly, highlighting the need for accurate legislative timelines.
        - Q59: Which one of the following revolts was made famous by Bankim Chandra Chatterjee in his novel ‘Anand Math’?
            - Options:
                (a) Bhil uprising
                (b) Rangpur and Dinapur uprising
                (c) Bishnupur and Birbhum rebellion
                (d) Sanyasi rebellion
            - Answer:
                (d) Sanyasi rebellion
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Bankim Chandra Chatterjee’s novel *Anand Math* (1882) fictionalized the Sanyasi Rebellion (1760s-1800s), a series of uprisings by sanyasis (Hindu ascetics) and fakirs (Muslim mendicants) in Bengal against British revenue policies and restrictions on religious practices.
                    - The novel, set during the 1770 Bengal famine, portrays sanyasi rebels as proto-nationalists, with the song *Vande Mataram* inspiring later freedom movements.
                    - The Sanyasi Rebellion involved figures like Majnu Shah and Bhabani Pathak, challenging British control in areas like Murshidabad and Rangpur.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Bhil uprising:
                        - Incorrect—Bhil revolts (1818-1840s) in Western India were unrelated to *Anand Math*.
                    - (b) Rangpur and Dinapur uprising:
                        - Incorrect—Rangpur (1783) saw peasant unrest, but not central to *Anand Math*; Dinapur had no major uprising linked to the novel.
                    - (c) Bishnupur and Birbhum rebellion:
                        - Incorrect—These areas saw minor revolts, but *Anand Math* focuses on sanyasi-led resistance.
                - Other Relevant Pre-1857 Revolts:
                    - Fakir Rebellion:
                        - Allied with Sanyasi Rebellion, led by fakirs like Majnu Shah.
                    - Chuar Rebellion (1760s-1800s):
                        - Bengal tribal uprising, contemporary to Sanyasi Rebellion.
                    - Paika Rebellion (1817):
                        - Odisha’s anti-British revolt, similar proto-nationalist spirit.
                - Significance of *Anand Math*:
                    - The novel transformed the Sanyasi Rebellion into a symbol of anti-colonial resistance, inspiring early nationalists and the Swadeshi movement through *Vande Mataram*.
                    - It reflects Bankim’s role in Bengal Renaissance, blending literature with nationalist fervor (Q52).
                - Related Figures:
                    - Bankim Chandra Chatterjee:
                        - Author of *Anand Math*, shaped nationalist consciousness.
                    - Majnu Shah:
                        - Fakir leader, key in Sanyasi Rebellion.
                    - Bhabani Pathak:
                        - Sanyasi leader, fictionalized in *Anand Math*.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on pre-1857 revolts (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Sanyasi Rebellion), Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Bankim), or nationalist literature (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on *Vande Mataram*).
                    - Reflects early anti-colonial resistance, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The Sanyasi Rebellion, immortalized in Bankim’s *Anand Math*, symbolizes early resistance to British rule, inspiring nationalist movements through its literary legacy.
        - Q60: With reference to the ‘revolt of the year’ who of the following was betrayed by a friend, captured and put to death by the British?
            - Options:
                (a) Nana Sahib
                (b) Kunwar Singh
                (c) Khan Bahadur Khan
                (d) Tatya Tope
            - Answer:
                (d) Tatya Tope
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The ‘revolt of the year’ refers to the 1857 Revolt. Tatya Tope, a key leader under Nana Saheb, was betrayed by his friend Man Singh, a Gwalior noble, in 1859.
                    - After leading guerrilla campaigns in Central India post-Kanpur and Jhansi, Tatya was captured by the British in April 1859 and executed, marking a significant blow to the revolt.
                    - His betrayal by Man Singh, who sought British favor, exemplifies the internal divisions exploited by the British to suppress the uprising.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Nana Sahib:
                        - Incorrect—Nana Sahib fled to Nepal after Kanpur’s defeat (1858); no record of betrayal or execution.
                    - (b) Kunwar Singh:
                        - Incorrect—Kunwar Singh died of battle wounds in 1858 in Bihar, not betrayed or captured (Q50).
                    - (c) Khan Bahadur Khan:
                        - Incorrect—Khan Bahadur Khan, Rohilkhand’s rebel leader, was executed in 1860, but no clear evidence of betrayal by a friend.
                - Other Relevant 1857 Revolt Betrayals:
                    - Siraj-ud-Daulah (1757):
                        - Betrayed by Mir Jafar at Plassey, a precursor to 1857 tactics (Q49).
                    - Bahadur Shah Zafar:
                        - Betrayed by allies, exiled to Burma, not executed.
                    - Rani Lakshmibai:
                        - Died in battle (1858), no betrayal recorded (Q51).
                - Significance of Tatya Tope’s Execution:
                    - Tatya Tope’s capture and death weakened the revolt’s momentum, highlighting British strategies of leveraging local rivalries to crush resistance.
                    - His guerrilla warfare inspired later nationalist movements, cementing his legacy as a 1857 hero.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Tatya Tope:
                        - Guerrilla leader, key in Central India’s revolt.
                    - Man Singh:
                        - Gwalior noble, betrayed Tatya for British favor.
                    - Nana Sahib:
                        - Tatya’s ally, led Kanpur uprising.
                    - Lord Canning:
                        - Viceroy, oversaw revolt suppression (Q61).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Tatya Tope), rebel leadership (e.g., Q76 [2006] on betrayals), or British tactics (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on divide-and-rule).
                    - Reflects the internal challenges of the 1857 Revolt, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Tatya Tope, betrayed by Man Singh and executed in 1859, was a pivotal 1857 Revolt leader, whose death underscored British exploitation of local divisions.
        - Q61: Who was the Governor-General of India during the Sepoy Mutiny?
            - Options:
                (a) Lord Canning
                (b) Lord Dalhousie
                (c) Lord Hardinge
                (d) Lord Lytton
            - Answer:
                (a) Lord Canning
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The Sepoy Mutiny (1857 Revolt) occurred under Lord Canning, Governor-General (1856-1862), who became the first Viceroy after the Government of India Act, 1858 (Q58).
                    - Canning managed the British response, suppressing the revolt through military campaigns and policies like the “Clemency Canning” approach, offering leniency to some rebels.
                    - His tenure saw the transition to Crown rule, addressing the revolt’s causes like annexation and sepoy grievances.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) Lord Dalhousie (1848-1856):
                        - Incorrect—Dalhousie’s policies (e.g., Doctrine of Lapse) sparked the revolt, but he left before it began (Q48).
                    - (c) Lord Hardinge (1844-1848):
                        - Incorrect—Hardinge preceded Dalhousie, unrelated to 1857 (Q47).
                    - (d) Lord Lytton (1876-1880):
                        - Incorrect—Lytton’s tenure was post-revolt, marked by the Vernacular Press Act (Q53).
                - Other Relevant Governor-Generals During Revolts:
                    - Lord Bentinck (1828-1835):
                        - Pre-revolt, introduced social reforms like Sati ban (Q52).
                    - Lord Ellenborough (1842-1844):
                        - Pre-revolt, focused on military campaigns, not 1857.
                    - Lord Ripon (1880-1884):
                        - Post-revolt, repealed Vernacular Press Act (Q53).
                - Significance of Canning’s Role:
                    - Canning’s handling of the 1857 Revolt, balancing suppression with clemency, stabilized British rule and facilitated the shift to direct Crown governance.
                    - His policies shaped post-revolt colonial administration, addressing grievances while reinforcing British authority.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Canning:
                        - First Viceroy, managed 1857 crisis.
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Predecessor, whose annexations fueled revolt (Q48).
                    - John Lawrence:
                        - Punjab’s administrator, aided Canning in revolt suppression.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Canning), colonial governance (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Viceroys), or post-revolt reforms (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Crown rule).
                    - Reflects the pivotal role of Viceroys in crisis management, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Lord Canning, Governor-General during the 1857 Revolt, oversaw its suppression and the transition to Crown rule, shaping colonial India’s post-revolt trajectory.
