        - Q62: Who among the following started the newspaper Shome Prakash?
            - Options:
                (a) Dayanand Saraswati
                (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
                (c) Raja Rammohan Roy
                (d) Surendranath Banerjee
            - Answer:
                (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - *Shome Prakash*, a Bengali weekly newspaper, was started by Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar in 1858 to promote social reforms, education, and nationalist ideas.
                    - Vidyasagar, a key figure in the Bengal Renaissance, used the newspaper to advocate for widow remarriage, women’s education, and critique colonial policies, aligning with his reformist initiatives like the Bethune School (Q52).
                    - Published in Bengali, it reached a wide audience, fostering public discourse in colonial India.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Dayanand Saraswati:
                        - Incorrect—Saraswati founded the Arya Samaj (1875) and wrote *Satyarth Prakash*, not a newspaper.
                    - (c) Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Incorrect—Roy started newspapers like *Sambad Kaumudi* (1821), but not *Shome Prakash*.
                    - (d) Surendranath Banerjee:
                        - Incorrect—Banerjee was associated with *The Bengalee* (1879), not *Shome Prakash* (Q33).
                - Other Relevant Nationalist Newspapers:
                    - Sambad Kaumudi (1821):
                        - Roy’s newspaper, promoted social reforms.
                    - The Bengalee (1879):
                        - Banerjee’s platform for nationalist agitation.
                    - Amrita Bazar Patrika (1868):
                        - Bengali-English newspaper, supported reforms (Q53).
                - Significance of Shome Prakash:
                    - *Shome Prakash* amplified Vidyasagar’s reformist agenda, mobilizing public opinion on issues like widow remarriage and education, contributing to the Bengal Renaissance.
                    - It reflects the role of vernacular press in fostering early nationalism, a precursor to the Indian National Congress.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar:
                        - Social reformer, founded *Shome Prakash* (Q52).
                    - Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Pioneered reformist journalism with *Sambad Kaumudi*.
                    - Surendranath Banerjee:
                        - Nationalist leader, used *The Bengalee* for political activism.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Vidyasagar), nationalist press (e.g., Q76 [2006] on vernacular media), or social reforms (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on widow remarriage).
                    - Reflects the role of print media in colonial India’s socio-political awakening, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar’s *Shome Prakash* was a vital platform for social reform and early nationalism, highlighting the Bengal Renaissance’s intellectual vigor.
        - Q63: The ruler of which one of the following States was removed from power by the British on the pretext of misgovernance?
            - Options:
                (a) Awadh
                (b) Jhansi
                (c) Nagpur
                (d) Satara
            - Answer:
                (a) Awadh
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Awadh was annexed by the British in 1856 under Lord Dalhousie, who cited “misgovernance” by Nawab Wajid Ali Shah as the pretext, claiming administrative inefficiency and corruption.
                    - Unlike Jhansi, Nagpur, and Satara, which were annexed via the Doctrine of Lapse (Q48), Awadh’s annexation was justified on governance grounds, leading to widespread discontent and fueling the 1857 Revolt.
                    - The annexation violated earlier treaties with Awadh, a loyal British ally, sparking resentment among its elites and sepoys.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (b) Jhansi:
                        - Incorrect—Annexed in 1853 via Doctrine of Lapse, not misgovernance (Q48).
                    - (c) Nagpur:
                        - Incorrect—Annexed in 1854 via Doctrine of Lapse, not misgovernance.
                    - (d) Satara:
                        - Incorrect—Annexed in 1848 via Doctrine of Lapse, not misgovernance.
                - Other Relevant British Annexations:
                    - Mysore (1831-1881):
                        - Temporarily taken over for misgovernance, restored later.
                    - Punjab (1849):
                        - Annexed after Second Sikh War, not misgovernance (Q45).
                    - Berar (1853):
                        - Leased from Hyderabad, not directly misgovernance-based.
                - Significance of Awadh’s Annexation:
                    - Awadh’s annexation, justified by misgovernance, alienated its taluqdars and sepoys, making Lucknow a major 1857 Revolt center under Begum Hazrat Mahal (Q51).
                    - It exemplifies British expansionist tactics beyond the Doctrine of Lapse, contributing to anti-colonial sentiment.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Architect of Awadh’s annexation, cited misgovernance (Q48).
                    - Wajid Ali Shah:
                        - Last Nawab of Awadh, deposed in 1856.
                    - Begum Hazrat Mahal:
                        - Led Awadh’s 1857 resistance post-annexation.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on 1857 Revolt (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Awadh), British annexation (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Dalhousie), or princely states (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on misgovernance).
                    - Reflects British territorial expansion strategies, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Awadh’s annexation in 1856 on the pretext of misgovernance, distinct from Doctrine of Lapse, fueled the 1857 Revolt, highlighting British manipulative tactics.
        - Q64: The First Factory Act restricting the working hours of women and children and authorizing local government to make necessary rules was adopted during whose time?
            - Options:
                (a) Lord Lytton
                (b) Lord Bentinck
                (c) Lord Ripon
                (d) Lord Canning
            - Answer:
                (c) Lord Ripon
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - The First Factory Act, 1881, was enacted during Lord Ripon’s tenure (1880-1884), restricting working hours for women and children in factories and authorizing local governments to frame safety and labor regulations.
                    - Influenced by British labor reforms and Indian labor unrest, the Act limited child labor (under 12) to 9 hours daily and introduced basic workplace safety, a precursor to later acts like the 1934 Factories Act (Q37).
                    - Ripon’s liberal policies, including the Ilbert Bill and Vernacular Press Act repeal, complemented this labor reform (Q43, Q53).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Lord Lytton (1876-1880):
                        - Incorrect—Lytton focused on press censorship (Q53), not labor reforms.
                    - (b) Lord Bentinck (1828-1835):
                        - Incorrect—Bentinck’s era preceded industrial labor issues; he focused on social reforms like Sati (Q52).
                    - (d) Lord Canning (1856-1862):
                        - Incorrect—Canning managed the 1857 Revolt, not factory legislation (Q61).
                - Other Relevant Colonial Labor Reforms:
                    - Factories Act, 1891:
                        - Expanded 1881 Act, introduced weekly holidays.
                    - Whitley Commission (1929):
                        - Investigated labor conditions, led to 1934 Act (Q37).
                    - Trade Unions Act, 1926:
                        - Legalized unions, built on earlier labor reforms.
                - Significance of the 1881 Factory Act:
                    - The Act marked the beginning of labor welfare legislation in colonial India, addressing exploitative factory conditions and setting a precedent for future reforms.
                    - It reflects Ripon’s progressive governance, balancing industrial growth with worker rights, though limited in enforcement.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Ripon:
                        - Liberal Viceroy, oversaw 1881 Act’s adoption (Q43).
                    - Sorabji Shapurji Bengalee:
                        - Indian reformer, advocated factory labor reforms.
                    - N.M. Joshi:
                        - Later labor leader, contextual to post-1881 reforms (Q37).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial labor policies (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on factory acts), liberal reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Ripon), or industrialization (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on labor welfare).
                    - Reflects the colonial response to industrial labor issues, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The First Factory Act of 1881, enacted under Lord Ripon, restricted women and child labor hours, marking a pioneering step in colonial labor welfare.
        - Q65: Who among the following Europeans were the last to come to pre-independence India as traders?
            - Options:
                (a) Dutch
                (b) English
                (c) French
                (d) Portuguese
            - Answer:
                (c) French
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - European traders arrived in India in the following order: Portuguese (1498, Vasco da Gama), Dutch (1602, Dutch East India Company), English (1600, English East India Company), French (1664, French East India Company).
                    - The French were the last to establish trading presence, setting up their first factory at Surat in 1668 and later at Pondicherry (1674), focusing on the Coromandel and Bengal trade.
                    - By the 18th century, French influence waned after defeats like the Battle of Wandiwash (1760), but they were the last to arrive as traders (Q56).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Dutch:
                        - Incorrect—Dutch arrived in 1602, establishing Pulicat factory by 1610 (Q39).
                    - (b) English:
                        - Incorrect—English arrived in 1600, with Surat factory by 1612 (Q38).
                    - (d) Portuguese:
                        - Incorrect—Portuguese were the first, arriving in 1498, capturing Goa by 1510 (Q38).
                - Other Relevant European Trading Milestones:
                    - Portuguese Calicut (1498):
                        - First European trade post, pre-dated others.
                    - Dutch Negapatam (1658):
                        - Expanded trade post-Pulicat (Q39).
                    - English Masulipatam (1611):
                        - Early South India factory (Q38).
                - Significance of French Arrival:
                    - The French, as the last European traders, intensified competition in India, leading to conflicts like the Carnatic Wars, though their late entry limited their dominance compared to the English.
                    - Their trading ventures shaped colonial rivalries, influencing British consolidation post-1760.
                - Related Figures:
                    - François Caron:
                        - Founded French factory at Surat, 1668.
                    - Joseph François Dupleix:
                        - French Governor, expanded trade and influence (Q38).
                    - Vasco da Gama:
                        - Portuguese pioneer, contextual to early arrivals.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on European colonialism (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on French), Carnatic Wars (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Dupleix), or trade networks (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on European factories).
                    - Reflects the sequential European mercantile expansion in India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - The French, arriving last in 1664, were the final European traders in pre-independence India, shaping colonial rivalries despite their eventual decline.
        - Q66: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. Robert Clive was the first Governor-General of Bengal
                2. William Bentinck was the first Governor-General of India
            - Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1 only
                (b) 2 only
                (c) Both 1 and 2
                (d) Neither 1 nor 2
            - Answer:
                (b) 2 only
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statement:
                        - 2. William Bentinck was the first Governor-General of India (1828-1835), as designated by the Charter Act, 1833, which centralized authority over all British territories in India (Q42).
                    - Incorrect statement:
                        - 1. Robert Clive was not the first Governor-General of Bengal; Warren Hastings held that title (1773-1785) under the Regulating Act, 1773. Clive was Governor of Bengal (1757-1760, 1765-1767) but not Governor-General (Q55).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1 only:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is false; Hastings was the first.
                    - (c) Both 1 and 2:
                        - Incorrect—Only statement 2 is correct.
                    - (d) Neither 1 nor 2:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 2 is correct.
                - Other Relevant Governor-Generals:
                    - Warren Hastings (1773-1785):
                        - First Governor-General of Bengal, relevant to statement 1’s correction (Q55).
                    - Lord Cornwallis (1786-1793):
                        - Succeeded Hastings, introduced permanent settlement.
                    - Lord Dalhousie (1848-1856):
                        - Post-Bentinck, expanded British territories (Q48).
                - Significance of These Titles:
                    - The transition from Governor-General of Bengal (Hastings) to Governor-General of India (Bentinck) reflects the centralization of British authority, formalized by the 1833 Act.
                    - The misattribution to Clive tests UPSC candidates’ understanding of colonial administrative titles.
                - Related Figures:
                    - William Bentinck:
                        - First Governor-General of India, enacted social reforms (Q52).
                    - Warren Hastings:
                        - First Governor-General of Bengal, not Clive (Q55).
                    - Robert Clive:
                        - Governor of Bengal, key in Plassey victory (Q56).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial administration (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Governor-Generals), Charter Acts (e.g., Q76 [2006] on 1833 Act), or British expansion (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Clive).
                    - Reflects the evolution of British governance in India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - William Bentinck was the first Governor-General of India, while Warren Hastings, not Robert Clive, was the first Governor-General of Bengal, clarifying colonial administrative roles.
        - Q67: Which one of the following was the first fort constructed by the British in India?
            - Options:
                (a) Fort William
                (b) Fort St George
                (c) Fort St David
                (d) Fort St Angelo
            - Answer:
                (b) Fort St George
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Fort St George, built in 1639-1640 by the English East India Company in Madras (Chennai), was the first British fort in India, serving as a trading and administrative hub on the Coromandel Coast.
                    - Constructed under Andrew Cogan, it marked the English foothold in South India, predating other British forts and facilitating trade with Southeast Asia.
                    - It became the nucleus of British Madras Presidency, central to colonial expansion.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Fort William:
                        - Incorrect—Built in 1696 in Calcutta, later than Fort St George.
                    - (c) Fort St David:
                        - Incorrect—Built in 1690 at Cuddalore, post-Fort St George.
                    - (d) Fort St Angelo:
                        - Incorrect—Built by the Portuguese in 1505 at Kannur, not a British fort.
                - Other Relevant British Forts:
                    - Fort William (1696):
                        - Calcutta’s key fort, replaced earlier structures.
                    - Fort St David (1690):
                        - Strengthened British Coromandel presence.
                    - Bombay Castle (1660s):
                        - Acquired from Portuguese, developed later.
                - Significance of Fort St George:
                    - As the first British fort, Fort St George established a permanent English presence, enabling trade and military dominance in South India, rivaling Portuguese and Dutch bases.
                    - It laid the foundation for the Madras Presidency, shaping British colonial administration.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Andrew Cogan:
                        - English agent, oversaw Fort St George’s construction.
                    - Francis Day:
                        - Secured land for Fort St George from local rulers.
                    - Robert Clive:
                        - Later defended Madras, contextual to British forts (Q56).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on British colonialism (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on forts), Madras Presidency (e.g., Q76 [2006] on trade hubs), or European expansion (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Coromandel).
                    - Reflects the early British mercantile-military strategy in India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Fort St George, built in 1639-1640, was the first British fort in India, anchoring English trade and colonial power in Madras.
        - Q68: Who among the following wrote the book Bahubivah?
            - Options:
                (a) Raja Rammohan Roy
                (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
                (c) Pandita Ramabai
                (d) Rabindranath Tagore
            - Answer:
                (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Bahubivah, written by Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar in 1873, was a Bengali treatise critiquing polygamy among Hindu Kulin Brahmins, advocating for monogamy and social reform.
                    - Vidyasagar, a leading Bengal Renaissance reformer, used the book to challenge patriarchal practices, building on his efforts for widow remarriage (1856 Act) and women’s education (Bethune School, Q52).
                    - The work influenced public opinion, contributing to later legal reforms like the Hindu Marriage Act (1955) that banned polygamy.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Incorrect—Roy wrote on Sati and monotheism (Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin, 1804), not Bahubivah.
                    - (c) Pandita Ramabai:
                        - Incorrect—Ramabai authored The High-Caste Hindu Woman (1887), focusing on women’s oppression, not polygamy.
                    - (d) Rabindranath Tagore:
                        - Incorrect—Tagore wrote literary works like Gitanjali (1910), not reformist treatises.
                - Other Relevant Social Reform Writings:
                    - Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin (1804):
                        - Roy’s work on monotheism, addressed religious reform.
                    - Stri Purush Tulana (1882):
                        - Tarabai Shinde’s feminist critique, contemporary to Bahubivah.
                    - The High-Caste Hindu Woman (1887):
                        - Ramabai’s book on women’s issues, post-dated Vidyasagar.
                - Significance of Bahubivah:
                    - Bahubivah challenged orthodox Hindu practices, advancing the Bengal Renaissance’s push for gender equality and social justice.
                    - It fostered intellectual discourse, setting the stage for 20th-century legislative reforms against polygamy.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar:
                        - Author of Bahubivah, led anti-polygamy and widow remarriage campaigns (Q52).
                    - Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Early reformer, influenced Vidyasagar’s rationalist approach.
                    - Pandita Ramabai:
                        - Contemporary reformer, focused on women’s education and Christian reform.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Vidyasagar), social reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on gender issues), or women’s rights (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on polygamy).
                    - Reflects the 19th-century reformist movement against patriarchal practices, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar’s Bahubivah (1873) critiqued polygamy, reinforcing his legacy as a Bengal Renaissance leader in gender reform.

        - Q69: Match List-I with List-II and select the correct answer using the codes given below the lists:
            - List-I (Author):
                A. Bankimchandra
                B. Dinabandhu Mitra
                C. Premchand
            - List-II (Work):
                1. Shatranj ke Khilari
                2. Debi Chaudhurani
                3. Nil-Darpan
                4. Chandrakanta
            - Options:
                (a) A-2, B-4, C-1
                (b) A-3, B-4, C-2
                (c) A-2, B-3, C-1
                (d) A-3, B-1, C-4
            - Answer:
                (c) A-2, B-3, C-1
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct matches:
                        - A. Bankimchandra (Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay) – 2. Debi Chaudhurani (1870): A Bengali novel portraying a woman’s empowerment and resistance, reflecting Bankim’s nationalist themes, similar to Anand Math (Q59).
                        - B. Dinabandhu Mitra – 3. Nil-Darpan (1860): A Bengali play exposing the exploitation of indigo planters by British colonizers, sparking controversy and nationalist sentiment.
                        - C. Premchand – 1. Shatranj ke Khilari (1924): A Hindi-Urdu short story critiquing the decadence of Awadh’s nobility under British influence, later adapted by Satyajit Ray.
                    - Incorrect match:
                        - 4. Chandrakanta (1888): Written by Devaki Nandan Khatri, not listed in List-I, a Hindi novel known for its fantasy and romance, unrelated to the given authors.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) A-2, B-4, C-1:
                        - Incorrect—B-4 is wrong; Nil-Darpan, not Chandrakanta, is by Mitra.
                    - (b) A-3, B-4, C-2:
                        - Incorrect—A-3 (Bankim-Nil-Darpan) and B-4 (Mitra-Chandrakanta) are wrong; C-2 (Premchand-Debi Chaudhurani) is also incorrect.
                    - (d) A-3, B-1, C-4:
                        - Incorrect—A-3 (Bankim-Nil-Darpan), B-1 (Mitra-Shatranj), and C-4 (Premchand-Chandrakanta) are all wrong.
                - Other Relevant Literary Works:
                    - Anand Math (1882):
                        - Bankim’s novel, popularized Sanyasi Rebellion (Q59).
                    - Godan (1936):
                        - Premchand’s novel, depicted rural Indian struggles.
                    - Bangadarshan (1872):
                        - Bankim’s magazine, promoted nationalist literature.
                - Significance of These Works:
                    - Debi Chaudhurani and Nil-Darpan fueled nationalist consciousness during the Bengal Renaissance, critiquing colonial oppression and social norms.
                    - Shatranj ke Khilari highlighted the cultural decay under British indirect rule, reflecting Premchand’s realist critique of colonialism.
                    - The inclusion of Chandrakanta tests UPSC candidates’ knowledge of distinct literary traditions (Bengali vs. Hindi).
                - Related Figures:
                    - Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay:
                        - Author of Debi Chaudhurani, Bengal Renaissance novelist (Q59).
                    - Dinabandhu Mitra:
                        - Wrote Nil-Darpan, exposed indigo exploitation.
                    - Premchand:
                        - Authored Shatranj ke Khilari, pioneer of Hindi-Urdu realism.
                    - Devaki Nandan Khatri:
                        - Wrote Chandrakanta, relevant to incorrect match.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Bankim), colonial exploitation (e.g., Q76 [2006] on indigo), or Hindi literature (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Premchand).
                    - Reflects the role of literature in fostering anti-colonial and social reform movements, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Bankim’s Debi Chaudhurani, Mitra’s Nil-Darpan, and Premchand’s Shatranj ke Khilari correctly match, highlighting their contributions to nationalist and realist literature.

        - Q70: In collaboration with David Hare and Alexander Duff, who of the following established Hindu College at Calcutta?
            - Options:
                (a) Henry Louis Vivian Derozio
                (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar
                (c) Keshab Chandra Sen
                (d) Raja Rammohan Roy
            - Answer:
                (d) Raja Rammohan Roy
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Hindu College, Calcutta, was established in 1817 by Raja Rammohan Roy, David Hare, and other reformers, with support from Alexander Duff, to promote modern education combining Western and Indian knowledge.
                    - Roy, a pioneer of the Bengal Renaissance, aimed to foster rationalism and counter orthodox Hindu practices, collaborating with Hare (a philanthropist) and Duff (a missionary educator) to fund and design the curriculum.
                    - The college became a hub for intellectual movements like Young Bengal, influencing figures like Derozio (Q31).
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) Henry Louis Vivian Derozio:
                        - Incorrect—Derozio was a teacher at Hindu College (1826-1831), not a founder, known for the Young Bengal movement.
                    - (b) Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar:
                        - Incorrect—Vidyasagar supported education (Bethune School, Q52), but was not involved in Hindu College’s founding.
                    - (c) Keshab Chandra Sen:
                        - Incorrect—Sen, a later Brahmo Samaj leader, was active in the 1860s, post-dating the college’s establishment (Q31).
                - Other Relevant Educational Institutions:
                    - Calcutta Medical College (1835):
                        - Founded under Bentinck, advanced professional education.
                    - Bethune School (1849):
                        - Vidyasagar’s initiative for women’s education (Q52).
                    - Presidency College (1855):
                        - Evolved from Hindu College, became a university.
                - Significance of Hindu College:
                    - Hindu College laid the foundation for modern education in India, nurturing intellectuals who shaped nationalist and reformist movements, including the Brahmo Samaj and Indian National Congress.
                    - It marked a shift from traditional to secular education, challenging colonial and orthodox constraints.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Raja Rammohan Roy:
                        - Founder of Hindu College, Brahmo Samaj leader (Q52).
                    - David Hare:
                        - Philanthropist, co-founder, promoted secular education.
                    - Alexander Duff:
                        - Missionary, contributed to college’s curriculum.
                    - Henry Louis Vivian Derozio:
                        - Hindu College teacher, led Young Bengal (Q31).
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on Bengal Renaissance (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on Roy), educational reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on Hindu College), or nationalist movements (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on Young Bengal).
                    - Reflects the intellectual awakening in colonial India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Raja Rammohan Roy, with David Hare and Alexander Duff, founded Hindu College in 1817, fostering modern education and the Bengal Renaissance.

        - Q71: Consider the following statements:
            - List:
                1. The first telegraph line in India was laid between Kolkata (formerly Calcutta) and Diamond Harbour
                2. The first Export Processing Zone in India was set up in Kandla
            - Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
            - Options:
                (a) 1 only
                (b) 2 only
                (c) Both 1 and 2
                (d) Neither 1 nor 2
            - Answer:
                (c) Both 1 and 2
            - Analysis:
                - Historical Context:
                    - Correct statements:
                        - 1. The first telegraph line in India was laid in 1851 between Kolkata and Diamond Harbour under Lord Dalhousie, initiated by William O’Shaughnessy, enabling rapid communication for colonial administration (Q48).
                        - 2. The first Export Processing Zone (EPZ) in India was established in Kandla, Gujarat, in 1965, to promote export-oriented industries, a post-independence economic initiative under the Ministry of Commerce.
                - Why Not Others?:
                    - (a) 1 only:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 2 is also correct.
                    - (b) 2 only:
                        - Incorrect—Statement 1 is also correct.
                    - (d) Neither 1 nor 2:
                        - Incorrect—Both statements are correct.
                - Other Relevant Colonial and Post-Independence Developments:
                    - First Railway Line (1853):
                        - Bombay to Thane, under Dalhousie, contemporary to telegraph (Q48).
                    - Santa Cruz EPZ (1973):
                        - Second EPZ, followed Kandla, boosted electronics exports.
                    - Postal System Reform (1854):
                        - Dalhousie’s initiative, complemented telegraph network.
                - Significance of These Developments:
                    - The Kolkata-Diamond Harbour telegraph line revolutionized colonial communication, aiding British control during the 1857 Revolt and setting the stage for India’s telecom infrastructure.
                    - The Kandla EPZ marked India’s post-independence push for export-led growth, aligning with Nehruvian economic policies and global trade integration.
                - Related Figures:
                    - Lord Dalhousie:
                        - Oversaw telegraph and railway introductions (Q48).
                    - William O’Shaughnessy:
                        - Pioneered India’s telegraph system.
                    - Jawaharlal Nehru:
                        - Influenced post-independence economic policies, contextual to Kandla EPZ.
                - Broader Connection:
                    - Links to questions on colonial infrastructure (e.g., Q105 [2015-I] on telegraph), Dalhousie’s reforms (e.g., Q76 [2006] on railways), or post-independence economy (e.g., Q150 [2024-I] on EPZs).
                    - Reflects the technological and economic transitions in colonial and independent India, a key UPSC theme.
                - Key Takeaway:
                    - Both the first telegraph line (Kolkata-Diamond Harbour, 1851) and the first EPZ (Kandla, 1965) are correctly stated, highlighting colonial and post-independence advancements.
